Start here

Five ideas behind everything in this unit

If you finished Unit 1, 80% of this unit is “the BJT story again with a new control knob”. These five ideas show what is the same and what is different.

Idea 1 · A voltage-controlled valve

A BJT is a current-controlled valve: base current IB controls collector current. A FET is a voltage-controlled valve: the gate voltage VGS controls the drain current ID, and almost no current flows into the gate. Think of a garden hose with a clamp. You change the flow by squeezing (a voltage-like push on the clamp), and the clamp itself takes no water. That is why FET amplifiers have a huge input impedance.

Idea 2 · One word: transconductance

The BJT had β (current gain). The FET has gm (transconductance): how many mA of drain current you get per volt of gate voltage.

g_{m} = [[Δi_{D}|Δv_{GS}]]   (unit: siemens, mS = mA/V)

Every FET amplifier gain is just gm times a resistance. For a CS stage: Av = −gmRD′. If you remember only one formula in this unit, remember this one.

Idea 3 · Same four steps as Unit 1
  1. DC: find the Q-point (IDQ, VGSQ, VDSQ).
  2. Parameters at Q: compute gm (and rd or ro).
  3. AC equivalent: VDD → ground, capacitors → wires, transistor → small-signal model.
  4. Solve for Zi, Zo, Av.
Idea 4 · BJT ↔ FET dictionary
BJTFETRemember
Base, Collector, EmitterGate, Drain, SourceB→G, C→D, E→S
CE, CC, CBCS, CD, CGSame behaviour: CS inverts with gain; CD follows (gain < 1); CG non-inverting with low Zi
IC = βIBID = gmVGSGate current ≈ 0
hie (finite)RGS (huge) or ∞So Zi ≈ RG = R1∥R2
Zi ≈ kΩZi ≈ MΩFETs load the source far less
Idea 5 · What is different from a BJT
  • The FET is a majority-carrier device (only electrons in an n-channel). Less noise, no minority-carrier storage, very good as a switch.
  • The current law is a square law: ID ∝ (VGS − Vx)². A BJT is exponential. So FET gm is smaller than a BJT’s at the same current, and so is the gain. Trade: much higher input impedance.
  • FET parameters vary a lot from one device to another (IDSS, VP, VT), so the Q-point must be set by circuit design, not by trusting data-sheet numbers.
Topic 1

JFET: structure, working and characteristics

Asked: Summer 2019 Q3 OR (characteristics, 10 marks) · Elements of Electronics (construction, 7 marks)Prerequisite for JFET models and amplifiers

1.1 What is a JFET and how is it built?

Intuition

Take a bar of n-type silicon. Current can flow along it from source (S) to drain (D) like water along a pipe. Now diffuse p-type regions on the two sides and join them: this is the gate (G). The p–n junction between each gate and the bar is kept reverse biased. A reverse-biased junction grows an insulating depletion region. The more negative VGS, the thicker that insulating layer, and the narrower the n-channel left for current. The gate is a pinch valve on the pipe, and it draws only the tiny reverse leakage current of a diode.

1.2 Why the drain curve flattens: pinch-off

Hold VGS = 0 and raise VDS from zero. Two things happen at once:

  1. Ohmic region (small VDS): the channel is wide, so ID rises almost linearly with VDS. The JFET behaves like a resistor whose value is set by VGS. This is the region used when the JFET works as a switch (Topic 6).
  2. Pinch-off: VDS also reverse-biases the gate junction, more at the drain end. The depletion layers thicken there and the channel narrows towards the drain. At VDS = |VP| (for VGS = 0) the channel nearly closes at the drain end.
  3. Active (saturation) region: beyond pinch-off, the extra VDS is dropped across the short pinched section. The current is fixed by how narrow the channel is, which only VGS controls. So ID stays nearly constant when VDS rises. This flat region is the amplifier region: the JFET acts as a gate-controlled current source.
  4. Breakdown: at a large VDS the gate–drain junction avalanches and ID shoots up. Never operate there.

Key points on the curves. IDSS is the drain current with VGS = 0 in the active region (the maximum ID). VP = VGS(off) is the gate voltage that turns the channel completely off (negative for n-channel, positive for p-channel). The pinch-off locus VDS = VGS − VP separates ohmic from active.

n-channel vs p-channel

A p-channel JFET is the mirror image: all voltages and currents reverse sign. VGS is positive, VP is positive, VDD is negative, and ID flows from source to drain. The curves look the same when drawn with magnitudes. Say this in one line; the exam question on “N-channel and P-channel” wants both sketches.

1.3 The Shockley equation

I_{D} = I_{DSS}(1 − [[V_{GS}|V_{GS(off)}]])^{2}    for V_{GS(off)} ≤ V_{GS} ≤ 0
Inverse form: V_{GS} = V_{GS(off)}(1 − √[[I_{D}|I_{DSS}]])   (use this when you choose a Q-point)

The transfer curve is a half parabola. Check three points to sketch it fast: VGS = 0 gives IDSS; VGS = VP/2 gives IDSS/4; VGS = VP gives 0.

1.4 Biasing and the Q-point

An amplifier needs a Q-point in the active region with room to swing. With voltage-divider bias (the circuit your notes use), the gate is held at VG and the source resistor creates VS:

V_{G} = V_{DD}[[R_{2}|R_{1}+R_{2}]]    V_{S} = I_{D}R_{S}    V_{GS} = V_{G} − I_{D}R_{S}    V_{DS} = V_{DD} − I_{D}(R_{D}+R_{S})

Combine the bias line (VGS = VG − IDRS) with the Shockley curve. Their intersection is the Q-point. A bigger RS gives a flatter bias line and a more stable Q-point (negative feedback), exactly like RE in a BJT.

Why the gate voltage can be negative-looking

For an n-channel JFET we need VGS ≤ 0. In the divider circuit VG is positive, but VS = IDRS is more positive, so VGS = VG − VS comes out negative. That is the trick: the source is lifted above the gate.

10-mark answer skeleton: “Explain the characteristics of N-channel and P-channel JFET”
  1. Neat constructional sketch of n-channel JFET with labels S, D, G, depletion regions (2).
  2. Drain characteristics sketch: several VGS curves, ohmic region, pinch-off locus, active region, breakdown. Mark IDSS and VP (3).
  3. Explain each region in two lines (2).
  4. Transfer characteristic and Shockley equation (1).
  5. P-channel: same curves with all polarities reversed (1). Short definition of gm, rd, μ (1, full treatment in Topic 2).
Quick check 1: IDSS = 12 mA, VP = −6 V. ID at VGS = −3 V?

ID = 12(1 − (−3/−6))² = 12(0.5)² = 3 mA (one quarter of IDSS, as expected at VP/2).

Quick check 2: Why is the input impedance of a JFET so large?

The gate–channel junction is reverse biased, so only a tiny leakage current flows. That gives a resistance of 10⁸–10¹⁰ Ω.

Topic 2

JFET parameters and small-signal model

Asked: Summer 2019 Q3 OR — “define the terms gm, rd and μ”

2.1 The three parameters

ParameterDefinitionMeaning
gm transconductanceΔiD/ΔvGS at constant VDSSlope of the transfer curve. Control strength of the gate. Unit: S (mS = mA/V).
rd drain resistanceΔvDS/ΔiD at constant VGSInverse slope of the drain curve in the active region. Large (tens of kΩ) because curves are nearly flat.
μ amplification factor|ΔvDS/ΔvGS| at constant IDHow many volts of drain voltage one volt of gate voltage can cancel. Dimensionless.
μ = g_{m} · r_{d}

Why μ = gmrd. A change ΔvGS changes the current by gmΔvGS. To bring the current back we must change vDS so that ΔvDS/rd cancels it: ΔvDS = −gmrdΔvGS. Hence |ΔvDS/ΔvGS| = gmrd.

2.2 Getting gm from the Shockley equation

Differentiate ID = IDSS(1 − VGS/VP)² with respect to VGS:

g_{m} = [[dI_{D}|dV_{GS}]] = [[2I_{DSS}|−V_{P}]](1 − [[V_{GS}|V_{P}]]) = g_{m0}(1 − [[V_{GS}|V_{P}]])
g_{m0} = [[2I_{DSS}||V_{GS(off)}|]]    g_{m} = g_{m0}√[[I_{D}|I_{DSS}]]

So gm is largest at VGS = 0 and shrinks to zero at pinch-off. A JFET biased at a smaller current has less gain.

Worked check (from your notes)

IDSS = 20 mA, VGS(off) = −5 V. Find ID and gm at VGS = −4 V.

gm0 = 2(20 mA)/5 V = 8 mS. ID = 20(1 − 4/5)² = 20(0.2)² = 0.8 mA. gm = 8(1 − 4/5) = 1.6 mS. Check: 8√(0.8/20) = 8(0.2) = 1.6 mS ✓. Near pinch-off the JFET gives only a fifth of its maximum gm.

2.3 Why a voltage change becomes a voltage gain (physical picture)

Your notes use this example: ID = 2 mA, VDD = 20 V, RD = 6 kΩ, gm = 2 mS.

  1. No signal: VDS = 20 − 2 mA × 6 kΩ = 8 V.
  2. Add +50 mV at the gate: ΔID = gmΔVGS = 2 mS × 50 mV = 0.1 mA.
  3. That extra current drops more voltage in RD: ΔVo = −0.1 mA × 6 kΩ = −0.6 V. Drain falls from 8 V to 7.4 V.
  4. −50 mV at the gate: drain rises to 8.6 V. So Av = −0.6/0.05 = −12. The minus sign means the output is inverted.
A_{v} = −g_{m}R_{D} (= −2 mS × 6 kΩ = −12)

2.4 Small-signal equivalent circuit

Replace the JFET by (a) a very large resistance RGS between gate and source (the reverse-biased junction), (b) a current source gmvgs from drain to source, and (c) rd across drain–source.

Compare with the BJT hybrid-π: the input resistor rπ (a few kΩ) became RGS (hundreds of MΩ), gm has the same role, and rd is the same as ro.

10-mark answer skeleton
  1. Define gm, rd, μ with the “constant” condition and unit (3).
  2. Show μ = gmrd (1).
  3. Graphical interpretation: gm on transfer curve, rd on drain curve (2).
  4. Equation for gm from Shockley, gm0 (2).
  5. Small-signal circuit with RGS, gmvgs, rd (2).
Quick check: gm = 4 mS and rd = 50 kΩ. What is μ?

μ = 4 mS × 50 kΩ = 200.

Topic 3

Analysis of JFET CS, CD and CG amplifiers

Derive Zi, Zo, Av · 10 marks eachSame method as Unit 1: AC equivalent, then solve

3.0 The common recipe

  1. Draw the circuit with voltage-divider bias. RG = R1∥R2 is what the source sees at the gate.
  2. VDD → AC ground. Capacitors → short circuits.
  3. Replace the JFET by the model: RGS, gmvgs, rd.
  4. Find Zi (looking into the input), Zo (looking into the output with the source off and RL removed), Av = vo/vi.

3.1 Common-source (CS)

Intuition

Input at the gate, output at the drain, source is the common terminal. It is the FET version of the CE amplifier: big voltage gain, inversion, very high input impedance, medium output impedance. It is the “main” FET voltage amplifier.

(a) RS bypassed. The source is at AC ground.

Z_{g} = R_{GS}  ⇒  Z_{i} = R_{G}∥R_{GS} ≈ R_{G}
Z_{d} = r_{d}  ⇒  Z_{o} = R_{D}∥r_{d}  (R_{L} removed)
v_{o} = i_{d}(r_{d}∥R_{D}∥R_{L}), i_{d} = −g_{m}v_{i}  ⇒  A_{v} = −g_{m}(r_{d}∥R_{D}∥R_{L}) ≈ −g_{m}(R_{D}∥R_{L})

(b) RS not bypassed. Now the drain current flows through RS, so the source voltage rises and cancels part of the input. This is negative feedback (source degeneration).

v_{i} = v_{gs} + i_{d}R_{S} = v_{gs}(1 + g_{m}R_{S})  ⇒  v_{gs} = [[v_{i}|1 + g_{m}R_{S}]]
Z_{g} = [[v_{i}|i_{g}]] = R_{GS}(1 + g_{m}R_{S})  ⇒  Z_{i} = R_{G}∥Z_{g}
Z_{d} = r_{d}(1 + g_{m}R_{S}) + R_{S}  ⇒  Z_{o} = R_{D}∥Z_{d}
A_{v} ≈ −[[g_{m}(R_{D}∥R_{L})|1 + g_{m}R_{S}]] = −[[R_{D}∥R_{L}|R_{S} + 1/g_{m}]] ≈ −[[R_{D}∥R_{L}|R_{S}]] (if g_{m}R_{S} ≫ 1)
What the degeneration buys

Gain drops by the factor (1 + gmRS), but the gain becomes almost independent of gm, which varies a lot between devices. It also stabilises the bias, reduces distortion and raises Zo and the bandwidth. “Lose gain, gain stability” is the one-line summary.

Worked example 1 (notes): CS, RS not bypassed

gm = 2.5 mS, rd = 50 kΩ, RD = 6 kΩ, RS = 1 kΩ, RL = 12 kΩ, RG = 1 MΩ.

  1. Zi ≈ RG = 1 MΩ (RGS is far larger).
  2. Zd = rd + RS + gmRSrd = 50 + 1 + (2.5)(1)(50) = 176 kΩ. Zo = 6k ∥ 176k = 5.80 kΩ.
  3. RD∥RL = 6k ∥ 12k = 4 kΩ.
  4. Av = −(2.5)(4)/(1 + 2.5×1) = −10/3.5 = −2.86.

3.2 Common-drain (CD), the source follower

Intuition

Input at the gate, output at the source, drain at AC ground (the supply). The output copies the input, only about 0.7 to 0.99 of it. If the gate rises 1 V, the source rises almost 1 V and VGS barely changes. The device acts as a buffer: it presents a huge impedance to the source and a small one to the load.

Let RS′ = rd∥RS∥RL. The controlled current gmvgs flows into the source node through RS′:

v_{o} = g_{m}v_{gs}R_{S}′    v_{i} = v_{gs} + v_{o} = v_{gs}(1 + g_{m}R_{S}′)
A_{v} = [[g_{m}R_{S}′|1 + g_{m}R_{S}′]]  (0 < A_{v} < 1, in phase)    Z_{i} = R_{G}∥Z_{g} ≈ R_{G}, Z_{g} ≈ R_{GS}(1 + g_{m}R_{S}′)

Output impedance. Set vi = 0 and look into the source. Any rise in the source voltage reduces vgs, so the device “fights back” like a resistor 1/gm:

Z_{s} ≈ [[1|g_{m}]]    Z_{o} = R_{S}∥Z_{s} ≈ R_{S}∥[[1|g_{m}]]
Worked example 2 (notes): CD

gm = 4 mS, rd = 40 kΩ, RS = 2.2 kΩ, RL = 4.7 kΩ, RG = 1 MΩ.

  1. Zi ≈ 1 MΩ.
  2. Zs ≈ 1/gm = 250 Ω. Zo = 2.2k ∥ 250 Ω = 224.5 Ω.
  3. RS′ = 40k ∥ 2.2k ∥ 4.7k = 1.444 kΩ.
  4. Av = (4)(1.444)/(1 + 4×1.444) = 5.78/6.78 = +0.85.

3.3 Common-gate (CG)

Intuition

Input at the source, output at the drain, gate at AC ground through a bypass capacitor. The signal pushes straight into the source, where the resistance is only about 1/gm, so Zi is low. When the source goes up, VGS goes down, the drain current falls, and the drain voltage rises. So there is no inversion. Used where a low input impedance or a wide bandwidth is needed (like the CB stage).

Z_{i} = R_{S}∥[[1|g_{m}]] ≈ [[1|g_{m}]]    Z_{o} = R_{D}∥r_{d}    A_{v} = g_{m}(r_{d}∥R_{D}∥R_{L}) ≈ g_{m}(R_{D}∥R_{L})

If the gate bypass capacitor is missing the gate follows the source through RG and RGS, so vgs = −viRGS/(RG+RGS) and the gain falls by that same fraction; Zi ≈ (1/gm)(RG+RGS)/RGS.

Worked example 3 (notes): CG

gm = 5 mS, rd = 60 kΩ, RS = 1 kΩ, RD = 4.7 kΩ, RL = 10 kΩ.

  1. Zs = 1/gm = 200 Ω. Zi = 1k ∥ 200 = 166.7 Ω.
  2. Zo = 4.7k ∥ 60k = 4.36 kΩ.
  3. RD(ac) = 60k ∥ 4.7k ∥ 10k = 3.036 kΩ. Av = 5 mS × 3.036 kΩ = +15.18.

3.4 Comparison (the table to memorise)

CSCDCG
Input / outputgate / draingate / sourcesource / drain
ZiHigh (RG)Very high (RG)Low (≈ 1/gm)
ZoMedium (RD∥rd)Low (≈ 1/gm)Medium-high (RD∥rd)
Av−gm(RD∥RL), high, inverting< 1, in phase+gm(RD∥RL), in phase
UseVoltage amplifierBuffer, impedance matchingWideband / high-frequency, current buffer
BJT twinCECCCB
10-mark answer skeleton: “Analyse the CS JFET amplifier”
  1. Circuit with divider bias, C1, CS, C3, RL (2).
  2. AC equivalent using the small-signal model (2).
  3. Zi and Zo derivation (bypassed or unbypassed, as the question says) (3).
  4. Av derivation and note on the 180° phase (2).
  5. One-line conclusion with typical values (1).

For CD and CG use the same layout. The key sentence for CD is vgs = vi − vo. For CG it is vgs = −vi.

Quick check 1: CS stage, gm = 3 mS, RD = 4 kΩ, RL = 4 kΩ, RS bypassed. Av?

RD∥RL = 2 kΩ, so Av = −3 × 2 = −6.

Quick check 2: same stage but RS = 500 Ω unbypassed.

Av = −6/(1 + 3 × 0.5) = −6/2.5 = −2.4.

Quick check 3: why can’t a source follower have gain above 1?

vo = gmRS′ vgs and vi = vgs + vo = vgs(1 + gmRS′). The denominator is always larger than the numerator.

Topic 4

Frequency response of JFET amplifiers

Not in your Unit 2 notes: standard theory, same ideas as BJT Topic 8

4.1 Why the gain falls at both ends

Intuition

In the mid-band, capacitors look like wires or like open circuits, and the answer is a flat gain Av = −gmRL′. Two kinds of capacitor spoil that:

  • Big external capacitors (C1, CS, C3) are wires only if the frequency is high enough. At low frequency they pass less signal, so the gain falls. That sets the lower cut-off f1.
  • Tiny internal capacitances (Cgs, Cgd, Cds, plus wiring) are open circuits at low frequency but leak signal away at high frequency. That sets the upper cut-off f2.

The band between f1 and f2, where the gain stays within 3 dB (0.707×) of the mid-band value, is the bandwidth.

4.2 High-frequency model

The Miller effect in one paragraph

Cgd connects the input (gate) to the output (drain), and the drain moves in opposite direction by A times as much. So when the gate rises by 1 V, the voltage across Cgd changes by (1 + A) V, and the capacitor must supply (1 + A) times the charge. From the input it looks like a capacitor (1 + A) times bigger.

C_{i} = C_{gs} + C_{gd}(1 + g_{m}R_{L}′)    C_{o} = C_{ds} + C_{gd}(1 + [[1|g_{m}R_{L}′]]) ≈ C_{ds} + C_{gd}
f_{in} = [[1|2π R_{sig} C_{i}]]    f_{out} = [[1|2π R_{L}′ C_{o}]]    f_{2} ≈ [[1|√(1/f_{in}² + 1/f_{out}²)]]

Rsig is the resistance the gate sees: rs∥RG (small, so the input pole is usually the first to bite). Higher gain → larger Miller capacitance → lower f2.

Unity-gain frequency: fT = gm / [2π(Cgs + Cgd)]. It tells you where the intrinsic device stops amplifying.

4.3 Low-frequency poles

CapacitorSees resistancePole
C1 (input coupling)rs + RGfC1 = 1/[2π(rs+RG)C1]
CS (source bypass)RS ∥ 1/gmfCS = (1+gmRS)/(2πRSCS)
C3 (output coupling)RD + RLfC3 = 1/[2π(RD+RL)C3]

The overall f1 is a little larger than the biggest of the three. A fast estimate is √(fC1² + fCS² + fC3²). The source bypass capacitor is normally the culprit because it sees a low resistance (about 1/gm). That is why design rules give CS the biggest value.

4.4 Worked example

gm = 3 mS, RL′ = 4 kΩ, source seen at gate 50 kΩ, Cgs = 5 pF, Cgd = 2 pF, Cds = 1 pF. C1 = 0.01 µF (sees 1 MΩ), CS = 10 µF (RS = 1 kΩ), C3 = 0.1 µF (sees 20 kΩ).

  1. Mid-band: A = 3 × 4 = 12 (21.6 dB).
  2. Ci = 5 + 2(1 + 12) = 31 pF. fin = 1/(2π × 50 k × 31 p) = 102.7 kHz.
  3. Co = 1 + 2(1 + 1/12) ≈ 3.2 pF. fout = 1/(2π × 4 k × 3.2 p) ≈ 12.6 MHz. So f2 ≈ 102.7 kHz, set by the input Miller pole.
  4. fC1 = 15.9 Hz, fCS = (1+3×1)/(2π × 1 k × 10 µ) = 63.7 Hz, fC3 = 79.6 Hz. f1 ≈ 103 Hz.
  5. Bandwidth ≈ 102.6 kHz. fT = 3 mS/[2π × 7 pF] ≈ 68 MHz.
10-mark answer skeleton
  1. CS circuit and typical frequency-response curve: mid-band, f1, f2, −3 dB, bandwidth (3).
  2. Low-frequency reasons and expressions for C1, CS, C3 (3).
  3. High-frequency model with Cgs, Cgd, Cds; Miller effect and Ci expression (3).
  4. Expressions for f2, comment on gain–bandwidth trade-off (1).
Quick check: why does a bigger gain reduce f2?

Miller capacitance Cgd(1 + gmRL′) grows with gain. A bigger capacitor makes the input pole 1/(2πRsigCi) lower.

Topic 5

Single-stage JFET CS amplifier design

Asked: Summer 2019 Q3 (design of a gate bias circuit, 10 marks)Method from your notes (Section 10)

5.1 The idea

You are given a gain, a supply and a load. You choose a Q-point and then find RD, RS, R1, R2. Three steps, in your notes’ order:

  1. Select the Q-point. Pick IDQ (below IDSS) and VDSQ. Then VGSQ = VP(1 − √(IDQ/IDSS)) and gm = gm0√(IDQ/IDSS).
  2. Find RD and RS. The DC loop gives RD + RS = K1 = (VDD − VDSQ)/IDQ. The gain with RS unbypassed is Av = (RD∥RL)/(RS + 1/gm). Put RS = K1 − RD, let K = K1 + 1/gm and A = |Av|:
A R_{D}² − [A(K − R_{L}) − R_{L}] R_{D} − A K R_{L} = 0

Solve the quadratic. Keep the root with 0 < RD < K1, then RS = K1 − RD. If no root fits, that gain is not possible with that Q-point.

  1. Gate divider. VG = VGSQ + IDQRS. With the required RG = R1∥R2:
R_{1} = R_{G}[[V_{DD}|V_{G}]]    R_{2} = R_{G}[[V_{DD}|V_{DD} − V_{G}]]

Finally round to standard values, recompute the DC point and check: active region, gain, and swing.

Your notes’ formulas for R1, R2 were corrected

The notes explicitly say the gate-divider formulas were corrected, so use the ones above. They follow from VG = VDDR2/(R1+R2) and RG = R1R2/(R1+R2). Divide the second by the first and you get R1 = RGVDD/VG.

5.2 Worked example (made to follow your method)

IDSS = 10 mA, VP = −4 V, VDD = 24 V, IDQ = 4 mA, VDSQ = 8 V, |Av| = 3, RL = 10 kΩ, RG = 1 MΩ.

  1. VGSQ = −4(1 − √0.4) = −1.47 V. gm0 = 2(10)/4 = 5 mS, gm = 5√0.4 = 3.16 mS.
  2. K1 = (24 − 8)/4 = 4 kΩ. K = 4 + 1/3.16 = 4.316 kΩ.
  3. Quadratic: 3RD² − [3(4.316 − 10) − 10]RD − 3(4.316)(10) = 0 → 3RD² + 27.05RD − 129.5 = 0 → RD = 3.46 kΩ.
  4. RS = 4 − 3.46 = 0.54 kΩ. Check gain: (3.46∥10)/(0.54 + 0.316) = 2.57/0.857 = 3.0 ✓.
  5. VG = −1.47 + 4 mA × 0.54 k = 0.69 V. R1 = 1 M × 24/0.69 = 34.7 MΩ, R2 = 1 M × 24/(24 − 0.69) = 1.03 MΩ.
Watch the sign of VG

If VG comes out negative, a divider to ground cannot make it. That means RS is too small, so the gain you asked for is too high for this Q-point. Reduce the gain or use self-bias. The widget below shows this warning.

5.3 The Summer 2019 question

Q3 (10 marks): design gate bias circuit with ID(max) = 3 mA, VDS(min) = 10 V, VDD = 25 V, VGS(off) = −6 V

The question does not give IDSS or the bias type, so state your assumptions. A clean way to answer (fixed gate bias):

  1. The largest drain current occurs at VGS = 0, so IDSS = ID(max) = 3 mA.
  2. The smallest VDS occurs at the largest current: VDS(min) = VDD − ID(max)RD → RD = (25 − 10)/3 mA = 5 kΩ (use 4.7 kΩ if you want a standard value: VDS(min) = 10.9 V).
  3. Choose the Q-point at VGS = VP/2 = −3 V for a good swing. IDQ = 3(1 − 0.5)² = 0.75 mA, VDSQ = 25 − 0.75 × 5 = 21.25 V (active, since VDS > VGS − VP = 3 V).
  4. Gate resistor RG = 1 MΩ (IG ≈ 0, so any large value). Gate supply VGG = −3 V.
  5. gm0 = 2(3)/6 = 1 mS; gm at Q = 0.5 mS, so Av = −gmRD ≈ −2.5.

If your syllabus means self-bias: RS = |VGSQ|/IDQ = 3 V/0.75 mA = 4 kΩ, and RD is then chosen so that RD + RS still satisfies VDS(min). Write the assumption in the first line and the examiner can follow your method.

10-mark answer skeleton
  1. Circuit diagram of the chosen bias (2).
  2. Choice of Q-point from the transfer curve and Shockley equation (2).
  3. RD, RS (and R1, R2 for divider bias) with the DC loop equation (4).
  4. Check: active region, gain, standard values (2).
Topic 6

The FET as a switch (JFET)

Not in your Unit 2 notes: standard theory. The MOSFET version (digital and power) is in Topics 12 and 13

6.1 Idea: a resistor you can switch

Intuition

In the ohmic region (small VDS) the JFET channel is just a resistor rds, and its value is set by the gate voltage. With VGS = 0 the channel is widest and rds is smallest, ON (typically 50 to 500 Ω). With VGS at or below VP the channel is pinched off and rds is enormous (10⁹ Ω or more), OFF. So the gate voltage is a control knob on a resistor, and the signal passes through the channel.

r_{ds} = [[r_{ds0}|1 − V_{GS}/V_{P}]],   r_{ds0} ≈ [[1|g_{m0}]] = [[V_{P}|2I_{DSS}]]  (V_{P} as a magnitude)

Derivation. For small VDS the triode equation reduces to ID ≈ (2IDSS/|VP|)(1 − VGS/VP)VDS. Then rds = VDS/ID gives the expression above.

6.2 Series and shunt switches

Series: [[V_{out}|V_{in}]] = [[R_{L}|R_{L} + r_{ds}]]    Shunt: [[V_{out}|V_{in}]] = [[r_{ds}|R_{s} + r_{ds}]]
  • Series, ON: rds(on) ≪ RL → Vout ≈ Vin. OFF: rds → ∞ → Vout ≈ 0.
  • Shunt, ON: the output is grounded, Vout ≈ 0. OFF: Vout ≈ Vin.
  • Chopper: a series and a shunt switch driven by opposite gate signals chop a DC input into a square wave (used in chopper amplifiers).

6.3 Why use a FET instead of a BJT as a switch?

FET switchBJT switch
No offset voltage across the ON switch (ideal resistor)VCE(sat) offset (0.1–0.3 V) always present
Gate draws no current: simple drive, high isolationNeeds base current
Majority carriers only: no storage time, fast and good for analog signalsStorage time ts limits speed
Higher ON resistance (a few ohms to hundreds)Lower ON resistance for power
Signal must stay small compared with |VP|, or rds changes along the waveform (distortion)—

Applications: analog multiplexers, sample-and-hold circuits, choppers, analog gates, voltage-controlled attenuators (rds as a variable resistor).

10-mark answer skeleton
  1. Drain characteristics, mark the ohmic region used for switching (2).
  2. rds equation and its dependence on VGS, ON and OFF conditions (3).
  3. Series and shunt switch circuits with output equations (3).
  4. Advantages over BJT, one application (2).
Quick check: IDSS = 10 mA, VP = −4 V. rds at VGS = 0 and at VGS = −2 V?

rds0 = 4/(2×10 mA) = 200 Ω. At −2 V: 200/(1 − 0.5) = 400 Ω.

Topic 7

MOSFET: structure, regions and characteristics

Asked: Summer 2019 Q4 OR — “difference between D-MOSFET and E-MOSFET with constructional diagram” (10 marks)

7.1 Enhancement MOSFET (E-MOSFET)

Intuition

Start with a p-type slab (the body). Put two n⁺ islands in it: source and drain. Between them there is no n-type path, just p-type material, so the two junctions back-to-back block current. Now put a thin oxide (SiO2) over the gap and a metal gate on it. Apply a positive gate voltage. The gate and the body form a capacitor, and the positive gate pulls electrons up to the surface and pushes holes away. Above a certain voltage, the threshold VT, the surface has more electrons than holes. It has been inverted into n-type: an induced channel joins source and drain. The more VGS exceeds VT, the more electrons, the lower the channel resistance. “Enhancement” means the channel is enhanced by the gate; at VGS = 0 the device is OFF.

Why the gate current is zero. The oxide is an insulator. The input is a capacitor, so IG ≈ 0 at DC and the DC input resistance is 10¹² Ω or more.

7.2 Depletion MOSFET (D-MOSFET): not in your notes

The D-MOSFET has a built-in n-channel, diffused under the oxide during manufacture. Current flows at VGS = 0 (IDSS). A negative VGS repels electrons and depletes the channel until it closes at VP. A positive VGS attracts more electrons, enhancing the channel beyond IDSS. So it works in both depletion and enhancement mode, and it follows the same Shockley equation as the JFET.

E-MOSFETD-MOSFET
Channel at VGS = 0None (normally OFF)Present (normally ON)
ConstructionNo physical channelChannel diffused between S and D
n-channel gate polarityVGS must be positive (> VT)Negative (depletion) or positive (enhancement)
Key parameterVT (threshold)VP and IDSS
EquationID = ½K(VGS − VT)²ID = IDSS(1 − VGS/VP)²
SymbolBroken (dashed) channel lineContinuous channel line
UseDigital logic, CMOS, power switching, most amplifiersSpecial analog, RF, gain-control stages

7.3 Regions of operation (E-MOSFET)

V_{OV} = V_{GS} − V_{T}  (overdrive voltage: how far the gate is above threshold)
RegionConditionPictureModel
CutoffVGS ≤ VTNo channelOpen switch, ID = 0
Triode (linear)VGS > VT and VDS < VOVContinuous channelVoltage-controlled resistor
Saturation (active)VGS > VT and VDS ≥ VOVChannel pinched off at the drain endVoltage-controlled current source
Triode: I_{D} = K_{n}[V_{OV}V_{DS} − [[V_{DS}²|2]]]    Saturation: I_{D} = [[K_{n}|2]]V_{OV}²(1 + λV_{DS})    K_{n} = μ_{n}C_{ox}(W/L)
Check the K convention first

Your notes write ID = ½K(VGS − VT)² with K = μnCoxW/L. Some books write ID = k(VGS − VT)² with k = K/2. A wrong convention doubles your answer. In the Summer 2019 numerical the symbol k′(W/L) is used, so state which equation you use. Topic 9 shows both.

Why saturation? When VDS reaches VOV, the gate-to-drain voltage falls to VT and the channel pinches off at the drain. More VDS just widens the pinched section; the current is set by VGS. The small slope that remains is channel-length modulation: the pinched section grows and shortens the effective channel, raising ID slightly (the λ term).

Worked problems from your notes

Problem 1. VT = 2 V, Kn = 4 mA/V², VGS = 3.5 V, VDS = 5 V. VOV = 1.5 V; 5 > 1.5 so saturation; ID = (4/2)(1.5)² = 4.5 mA.

Problem 2. VT = 1.8 V, VGS = 3.3 V, VDS = 0.7 V, Kn = 2 mA/V². VOV = 1.5 V; 0.7 < 1.5 so triode; ID = 2[1.5(0.7) − 0.7²/2] = 1.61 mA.

Problem 3. VT = 1 V, Kn = 1 mA/V², VGS = 4 V. Boundary VDS = 3 V; at VDS = 1 V: ID = 1[3(1) − 0.5] = 2.5 mA; saturation current = ½(1)(9) = 4.5 mA.

10-mark answer skeleton: “D-MOSFET vs E-MOSFET”
  1. Constructional diagram of E-MOSFET with S, G, D, oxide, n⁺ regions, p-substrate, induced channel (3).
  2. Constructional diagram of D-MOSFET with the built-in channel (3).
  3. Working of each in two or three lines: VT and induced channel vs depletion and enhancement mode (2).
  4. Comparison table: channel at VGS = 0, polarity, equations, symbols, applications; transfer curves (2).
Quick check: why can’t an E-MOSFET with VT = 2 V conduct at VGS = 1.5 V?

VGS is below threshold: the surface is not inverted, no channel forms. ID ≈ 0 (cutoff).

Topic 8

MOSFET models: large-signal and small-signal

8.1 Large-signal (DC) models

Used for the Q-point, the output swing and the switch. Choose the model by region:

RegionModel between D and SEquation
CutoffOpen circuitID = 0
TriodeResistor RDS(on) set by VGSID ≈ KnVOVVDS → RDS(on) ≈ 1/(KnVOV)
SaturationCurrent source controlled by VGS (gate draws no current)ID = ½KnVOV²

The triode resistance comes from dropping the VDS² term, which is valid when VDS ≪ VOV (that is the “small voltage across a closed switch” case).

Worked problem 5 (notes): large-signal Q-point

VDD = 12 V, RD = 2 kΩ, VT = 1 V, Kn = 2 mA/V², VGS = 3 V. VOV = 2 V. Assume saturation: IDQ = (2/2)(2)² = 4 mA. VDSQ = 12 − 4 × 2 = 4 V. Check: 4 V > VOV = 2 V ✓ saturation valid. Always check the assumption after solving.

8.2 Small-signal model

Where gm comes from. Write vGS = VGSQ + vgs in the saturation equation:

i_{D} = [[K_{n}|2]](V_{OV} + v_{gs})² = [[K_{n}|2]]V_{OV}² + K_{n}V_{OV}v_{gs} + [[K_{n}|2]]v_{gs}²

The first term is IDQ, the DC part. The second is linear in vgs: this is the signal. The third is distortion. It is negligible if vgs ≪ 2VOV. That is the meaning of “small signal”.

g_{m} = [[∂I_{D}|∂V_{GS}]] = K_{n}V_{OV} = [[2I_{DQ}|V_{OV}]] = √(2K_{n}I_{DQ})    r_{o} ≈ [[1|λI_{DQ}]]
i_{d} = g_{m}v_{gs} + [[v_{ds}|r_{o}]]    (if r_{o} is neglected, i_{d} ≈ g_{m}v_{gs})
MOSFET vs BJT: gm

BJT gm = IC/VT = IC/25 mV. MOSFET gm = 2ID/VOV, and VOV is typically 0.2 to 1 V, so gm is several times smaller at equal current. Cost of the huge input impedance and the square law.

Worked problem 6 (notes)

IDQ = 2 mA, VOV = 0.5 V, λ = 0.02 V⁻¹. gm = 2(2)/0.5 = 8 mS, ro = 1/(0.02 × 2 mA) = 25 kΩ. For vgs = 10 mV, vds = −0.2 V: id = 8 mS × 10 mV + (−0.2)/25 kΩ = 80 µA − 8 µA = 72 µA.

8.3 Using the model (same four steps)

  1. DC sources → AC ground. Large capacitors → short circuits.
  2. Replace the MOSFET by the model. Gate current = 0.
  3. CS gain: Av = −gmRD′ with RD′ = RD∥RL∥ro. With an unbypassed RS: Av = −gmRD′/(1 + gmRS) ≈ −RD′/RS.

Why negative: a positive vgs raises id, more current drops more voltage across RD, so the drain voltage falls: 180° inversion.

Worked problem 7 (notes): degeneration

gm = 5 mS, RD′ = 4 kΩ, RS = 200 Ω. gmRD′ = 20, 1 + gmRS = 2 → Av = −10. Bypassing RS would give −20.

10-mark answer skeleton
  1. Why a model is needed; large-signal vs small-signal (1).
  2. Large-signal models by region with equations (3).
  3. Derive gm (expansion above) and ro (3).
  4. Small-signal circuit with equation id = gmvgs + vds/ro (2).
  5. Small-signal condition vgs ≪ 2VOV (1).
Quick check: IDQ = 1 mA, Kn = 2 mA/V². gm?

gm = √(2 × 2 × 1) = 2 mS (VOV = 1 V).

Topic 9

Analysis of MOSFET amplifiers

Asked: Summer 2019 Q4 — Q-point of a voltage-divider-biased n-channel MOSFET (10 marks)

9.1 The MOSFET as a voltage amplifier (the picture)

Notes example: VDD = 12 V, RD = 8 kΩ, K = 2 mA/V², VT = 2 V, VGS = 3 V, vi = ±100 mV.

  1. Q: ID = ½(2)(3 − 2)² = 1 mA. VDS = 12 − 1 × 8 = 4 V.
  2. +100 mV: VGS = 3.1 V → ID = ½(2)(1.1)² = 1.21 mA → VDS = 12 − 1.21 × 8 = 2.32 V.
  3. −100 mV: VGS = 2.9 V → ID = 0.81 mA → VDS = 5.52 V.
  4. ΔVo = 5.52 − 2.32 = 3.2 V for ΔVi = 0.2 V → Av = −16 = −gmRD = −(2 mA/V)(8 kΩ) ✓.

Note the small asymmetry (the rise was 0.21 mA, the fall 0.19 mA): the square-law distortion in action.

9.2 Common-source

The AC equivalent is the JFET one (Topic 3) with RGS → ∞ and rd → ro. Using vg = vgs + gmvgsRS:

A_{vt} = [[v_{o}|v_{g}]] = −[[g_{m}(R_{D}∥R_{L})|1 + g_{m}R_{S}]]    v_{g} = v_{s}[[R_{G}|R_{G} + r_{s}]]
A_{v} = −[[g_{m}R_{D}′|1 + g_{m}R_{S}]]·[[R_{G}|R_{G} + r_{s}]] ≈ −[[R_{D}′|R_{S}]]   Z_{i} = R_{G}   Z_{o} = R_{D}∥r_{o}(1 + g_{m}R_{S}) ≈ R_{D}
Worked problem 9 (notes)

gm = 4 mS, RD = 6.8 kΩ, RL = 20 kΩ, RS = 470 Ω (unbypassed), RG = 100 kΩ, rs = 600 Ω.

RD′ = 5.075 kΩ. Avt = −(4)(5.075)/(1 + 4 × 0.47) = −20.3/2.88 = −7.05. RG/(RG + rs) = 0.994 → Av = −7.01. Zi = 100 kΩ, Zo ≈ 6.8 kΩ.

9.3 Common-drain

A_{vt} = [[g_{m}(R_{S}∥R_{L})|1 + g_{m}(R_{S}∥R_{L})]]    Z_{i} = R_{G}    Z_{o} ≈ [[1|g_{m}]]
Worked problem 10 (notes)

gm = 5 mS, RS = 2.2 kΩ, RL = 10 kΩ, RG = 220 kΩ. RS′ = 1.803 kΩ → Av = 9.015/10.015 = 0.90. Zi = 220 kΩ. Zo ≈ 1/gm = 200 Ω.

9.4 Common-gate

A_{v} = [[g_{m}(R_{D}∥R_{L})|1 + g_{m}(r_{s}∥R_{S})]]    Z_{i} ≈ [[1|g_{m}]]    Z_{D} = r_{o}[1 + g_{m}(R_{S}∥r_{s})], Z_{o} ≈ R_{D}∥Z_{D}
Worked problem 11 (notes)

gm = 6 mS, RD = 4.7 kΩ, RL = 10 kΩ, rs = 50 Ω, RS = 1 kΩ, ro = 50 kΩ. RD′ = 3.197 kΩ, rs∥RS = 47.62 Ω → Av = 19.18/(1 + 0.2857) = +14.92. Zi ≈ 1/gm = 166.7 Ω. ZD = 50k(1.2857) = 64.29 kΩ, Zo = 4.7k ∥ 64.29k = 4.38 kΩ.

AvZiZo
CS−gmRD′/(1+gmRS)RGRD∥ro(1+gmRS) ≈ RD
CDgm(RS∥RL)/(1+gm(RS∥RL))RG≈ 1/gm
CG+gmRD′/(1+gm(rs∥RS))1/gm≈ ro[1+gm(RS∥rs)]

9.5 Finding the Q-point of the divider-biased MOSFET (the exam numerical)

Summer 2019 Q4: R1 = 90 kΩ, R2 = 45 kΩ, RD = 5 kΩ, RS = 2 kΩ, VT = 1 V, k′(W/L) = 2 mA/V²

The paper does not print VDD. I used 12 V: it gives clean numbers and the paper’s R values sum to a 2:1 divider. If your copy shows another VDD, redo with the widget.

  1. VG = 12 × 45/135 = 4 V. IG = 0, so no loading of the divider.
  2. Bias line: VGS = VG − IDRS = 4 − 2ID (ID in mA).
  3. Device: ID = ½(2)(VGS − 1)² = (VGS − 1)².
  4. Put x = VGS − 1. Then x + 1 = 4 − 2x² → 2x² + x − 3 = 0 → x = 1 or x = −1.5. Reject −1.5 (it would put VGS below VT). So VGSQ = 2 V, IDQ = 1 mA.
  5. VDSQ = 12 − 1 mA × (5 + 2) kΩ = 5 V.
  6. Check: VOV = 1 V and VDS = 5 V > 1 V → saturation ✓.

If your course defines ID = k′(W/L)(VGS − VT)² (no ½), the same method gives IDQ = 1.125 mA and VDSQ = 4.125 V. Write the equation you use in the first line.

10-mark answer skeleton
  1. Circuit (1) and DC equivalent with VG from the divider (1).
  2. KVL on the gate–source loop and the saturation equation; solve the quadratic; reject the non-physical root (4).
  3. VDSQ from the drain loop (1).
  4. Check VDS ≥ VOV to confirm saturation (2).
  5. Final answer boxed with units (1).
Quick check: why is Zi = RG for CS and CD but 1/gm for CG?

In CS and CD the signal enters the insulated gate, which draws no current; only the bias resistors load the source. In CG the signal enters the source, where the device looks like a resistance 1/gm.

Topic 10

Frequency response of MOSFET amplifiers

Not in your Unit 2 notes (only a design rule with C4 appears). Same theory as JFET Topic 4

The picture is identical to the JFET (Topic 4): mid-band flat, falling at low frequency because of the coupling and bypass capacitors, falling at high frequency because of the internal device capacitances. Use the same high-frequency model with the MOSFET’s capacitances:

  • Cgs (gate–source) ≈ ⅔ WLCox + overlap. Cgd (gate–drain) is mainly the small overlap capacitance. Cdb, Csb are the junction capacitances to the body.
C_{i} = C_{gs} + C_{gd}(1 + g_{m}R_{L}′)    f_{2} ≈ [[1|2π R_{sig} C_{i}]]    f_{T} = [[g_{m}|2π(C_{gs} + C_{gd})]]
f_{1} ≈ √(f_{C1}² + f_{CS}² + f_{C3}²)    f_{C1} = [[1|2π(r_{s}+R_{G})C_{1}]]   f_{CS} = [[1 + g_{m}R_{S}|2πR_{S}C_{S}]]   f_{C3} = [[1|2π(R_{D}+R_{L})C_{3}]]
Where your notes touch this

In the 2N7000 design (Topic 11), the notes pick C1, C2, C3 from the rule XC ≤ 0.1R at fL = 20 Hz, and C4 = 1/[2πfH(RD∥RL)] at fH = 20 kHz. C4 is a small capacitor across the output that sets the upper cut-off of the design, so that the circuit’s own high-frequency roll-off is controlled and does not depend on the device capacitances.

Worked example (defaults of the widget)

gm = 7.5 mS, RL′ = 6.8 kΩ, source resistance 50 kΩ, Cgs = 6 pF, Cgd = 1.5 pF. A = 51 (34.2 dB). Ci = 6 + 1.5(52) = 84 pF → fin ≈ 37.9 kHz = f2 (the output pole is at 9.3 MHz). CS = 68 µF with RS = 1.5 kΩ gives fCS = (1 + 11.25)/(2π × 1.5 k × 68 µ) = 19.1 Hz, so f1 ≈ 19 Hz. A 51× gain with a 38 kHz bandwidth: the Miller multiplication is clear.

10-mark answer skeleton
  1. Frequency-response curve, mid-band, f1, f2, −3 dB, bandwidth (2).
  2. Low-frequency effect of C1, CS, C3 with expressions (3).
  3. High-frequency model with Cgs, Cgd, Miller effect, f2 (4).
  4. fT and gain–bandwidth remark (1).
Topic 11

Single-stage MOSFET CS amplifier design

From your notes: NMOS CS design with the 2N7000 (Av ≥ 50)

11.1 Specification

Av ≥ 50, vi = 50 mV, VDD = 12 V, R1∥R2 = 100 kΩ, 2N7000, very high RL, fL = 20 Hz, upper design point 20 kHz.

11.2 Step by step (with the “why”)

  1. Device constant. Data: VT = 2 V, ID(on) = 1 A at VGS(on) = 10 V. From ID = k(VGS − VT)²: k = 1/(10 − 2)² = 15.6 mA/V² (this is K/2 in the notes’ convention).
  2. Q-point from the gain. Av = gmRD and put VDSQ = 0.5VDD, so RD = 0.5VDD/IDQ. With gm = 2kVOV and IDQ = kVOV² this gives a neat result: VOV = VDD/Av = 12/50 = 0.24 V. So VGSQ = 2.24 V and IDQ = 0.0156 × 0.24² … in the notes they solve two equations to get the same VGSQ = 2.24 V, IDQ = 898.6 µA.
  3. RD and RS. RD = 6 V/898.56 µA = 6.67 kΩ → use 6.8 kΩ. Take 10% of VDD across RS for stability: RS = 1.2 V/898.56 µA = 1.33 kΩ → 1.5 kΩ (VRS = 1.35 V).
  4. Divider. VG = VS + VGS = 1.35 + 2.24 = 3.59 V. R2/(R1+R2) = 3.59/12 = 0.299; R1 = 100 k/0.299 = 334 kΩ → 390 kΩ; R2 = 100 k/0.701 = 142.7 kΩ → 150 kΩ.
  5. Capacitors from XC ≤ 0.1R at 20 Hz: C1 ≥ 1/(2π × 20 × 0.1 × 100 k) = 796 nF → 1 µF. C2 (bypass, RS = 1.5 k) ≥ 53 µF → 68 µF. C3 (RD = 6.8 k) ≥ 11.7 µF → 15 µF. C4 = 1/(2π × 20 k × 6.8 k) = 1.17 nF → 1.2 nF.
  6. Check with the real values. gm = 2(0.0156)(2.24 − 2) = 7.488 mS; Av = gmRD = 7.488 × 6.8 = 50.9 ✓ ≥ 50.
One subtlety in the notes

The notes call 15.6 mA/V² “K/2”. Then ID = 0.0156VOV² and gm = 2 × 0.0156 × VOV, consistent. Don’t confuse it with K = μnCoxW/L = 31.2 mA/V².

11.3 Second worked design (Problem 13)

VDD = 15 V, |Av| = 40, VT = 2 V, Kn = 10 mA/V² (ID = ½KnVOV²), R1∥R2 = 100 kΩ, fL = 50 Hz.

VOV = 15/40 = 0.375 V; IDQ = 5 × 0.375² = 0.703 mA; RD = 7.5 V/0.703 mA = 10.67 kΩ; RS = 1.5 V/0.703 mA = 2.13 kΩ; VG = 2.375 + 1.5 = 3.875 V → R1 ≈ 387 kΩ, R2 ≈ 135 kΩ; C1 ≥ 0.318 µF, C2 ≥ 14.9 µF, C3 ≥ 2.98 µF.

Try the widget with VDD = 15, Av = 40, ID(on) = 1000 mA, VGS(on) and VT chosen so that k = 5 mA/V² (e.g. VGS(on) − VT = 14.1 V) to reproduce Problem 13.

10-mark answer skeleton
  1. Circuit (1) and device data/constant k (1).
  2. Q-point: VOV = VDD/Av, IDQ (2).
  3. RD, RS with standard values (2).
  4. R1, R2 from VG and R1∥R2 (2).
  5. C1, C2, C3, C4 from the frequency rules (1).
  6. Check final gain with standard values (1).
Topic 12

MOSFET as a switch, NOT gates and the CMOS inverter

12.1 The switch

Intuition

A good switch is either a very high resistance (OFF) or a very low resistance (ON). The E-MOSFET does exactly this: VGS ≤ VT → no channel → open. VGS far above VT with small VDS → triode → a resistor RDS(on) = 1/(KnVOV) of a few milliohms to ohms. Saturation is the wrong state for an ON switch: VDS is large and the transistor wastes power. Amplifiers live in saturation; switches live in cutoff and deep triode.

Worked problem 8 (notes): low-side power switch

5 V supply, 100 Ω load, RDS(on) = 0.2 Ω. ID = 5/100.2 = 49.9 mA. VDS(on) = 49.9 mA × 0.2 = 9.98 mV. PD = ID²RDS(on) = 0.498 mW.

12.2 Resistor-load NOT gates and their problem

  • Vin LOW (0 V): VGS = 0 < VT, switch open, no current → Vout = +VDD (HIGH).
  • Vin HIGH (VDD): switch ON with resistance rDS, so Vout = VDD·rDS/(R + rDS) — a weak LOW, not exactly 0.
  • A p-channel version (source at +V) gives a weak HIGH: Vout = VDDR/(R + rDS).
  • The resistor also wastes power whenever the transistor is ON, and its large value makes the output rise slowly when charging a capacitive load.
Worked: design R for a required LOW (notes)

V = 5 V, K = 0.5 mA/V², VT = 1 V, VOL = 0.2 V. HIGH input (5 V) → triode. ID = ½(0.5)[2(5 − 1)(0.2) − 0.2²] = 0.25 × 1.56 = 0.39 mA. R = (5 − 0.2)/0.39 mA = 12.3 kΩ.

Problem 14: V = 5 V, R = 10 kΩ, rDS = 200 Ω → n-channel LOW = 5 × 200/10200 = 0.098 V; p-channel HIGH = 5 × 10000/10200 = 4.902 V; I = 0.490 mA; PR = I²R = 2.40 mW.

12.3 The CMOS inverter: the fix

VinPMOSNMOSVout
0 V (LOW)ON (VGS = −VDD < VTp)OFFVDD, full HIGH
VDD (HIGH)OFFON (VGS = VDD > VTn)0 V, full LOW
  • In each stable state one transistor is OFF, so no path from VDD to ground: static power ≈ 0.
  • Output levels are full rail (0 and VDD): no weak levels.
  • Power is used only while switching (charging CL): Pdyn = CLVDD²f.
  • Fast: CL is charged and discharged through low rDS of an ON transistor: t ≈ 0.69RonCL.
Worked problem 15 (notes)

VDD = 5 V, CL = 20 pF, f = 1 MHz. Static power = 0. Pdyn = 20 pF × 25 × 1 MHz = 0.50 mW. With Ron = 1 kΩ: t ≈ 0.69 × 1 k × 20 p = 13.8 ns.

10-mark answer skeleton
  1. MOSFET regions used as a switch: cutoff OFF, triode ON, why not saturation (2).
  2. Resistor-load NMOS inverter with working and the Vout equation, weak LOW (3).
  3. CMOS inverter circuit, truth/state table, working (3).
  4. Advantages: zero static power, full logic levels, speed; dynamic power formula (2).
Topic 13

Power MOSFET

13.1 Why it is built differently

Intuition

A small-signal MOSFET has source and drain side by side on the chip surface, so current runs sideways in a thin film: great for logic, but the current and voltage are limited. A power MOSFET puts the drain on the back of the chip, so current flows vertically through the whole chip thickness. Thousands of cells in parallel give a large current (big area → low RDS(on)). A lightly doped n⁻ drift layer sits between the body and the drain; it supports the blocking voltage: thicker layer = higher VDSS, but also higher RDS(on). That is the main trade-off.

The inherent p-body/n⁻ junction forms a body diode from source to drain. It conducts when the drain goes below the source (useful as a freewheel path, but with reverse-recovery loss).

13.2 Datasheet parameters

ParameterMeaningWhy it matters
VDSSMaximum drain–source blocking voltageMust exceed the highest drain voltage including spikes
IDContinuous / pulsed current ratingLimited by temperature, package
RDS(on)ON resistance at a specified VGSSets conduction loss; rises with temperature
VGS(th)Threshold at a small test currentNot the drive voltage. Drive at the VGS where RDS(on) is specified
Qg, QgdTotal gate charge, Miller chargeGate-drive current and switching speed
Ciss, Coss, CrssInput, output, reverse-transfer capacitanceSwitching dynamics
PD, TJ, RθPower, junction temperature, thermal resistanceCan the heat be removed?
SOASafe operating areaAllowed (VDS, ID, time) combinations

13.3 Gate charge and the Miller plateau

The gate is a capacitor, so every switching event needs charge. The turn-on sequence:

  1. Delay: the gate driver charges Cgs until VGS reaches Vth. Nothing happens to ID yet.
  2. Current rise: ID rises to the load current while VDS is still high (power is large: V × I overlap).
  3. Miller plateau: VGS stays almost constant while the driver charges Cgd (charge Qgd). The drain voltage falls rapidly here.
  4. Full enhancement: VGS rises to the drive level; RDS(on) reaches its final low value.
I_{G(avg)} ≈ Q_{g}f_{s}    P_{gate} ≈ Q_{g}V_{drive}f_{s}    (a low-impedance gate driver is needed to switch quickly)

13.4 Losses and temperature

P_{cond} ≈ D·I_{D}²R_{DS(on)}    E_{sw} ≈ ½V_{DS}I_{D}t_{sw}    P_{sw} ≈ f_{s}(E_{on} + E_{off})    P_{loss} = P_{cond} + P_{sw} + P_{gate}    T_{J} ≈ T_{A} + P_{loss}R_{θJA}
StateRegionDominant loss
OFFCutoffLeakage (small)
ONDeep triodeConduction: ID²RDS(on)
TransitionPasses through saturationSwitching loss: VDS·ID overlap
Worked example (widget defaults)

48 V, 20 A, RDS(on) = 10 mΩ (×1.6 hot), D = 0.5, fs = 100 kHz, tr+tf = 40 ns, Qg = 60 nC at 10 V. Pcond = 0.5 × 400 × 0.016 = 3.2 W. Psw = 100 k × ½ × 48 × 20 × 40 ns = 1.92 W. Pgate = 60 n × 10 × 100 k = 0.06 W. Total = 5.18 W. With RθJA = 12 °C/W and 40 °C ambient: TJ = 40 + 5.18 × 12 = 102 °C, safe below 150 °C.

Design trade-off in one line

A lower RDS(on) means a bigger die and a bigger Qg: you gain conduction loss but lose switching speed and drive power. Choose by comparing both loss terms at your frequency.

13.5 Power MOSFET vs power BJT (link back to Unit 1)

Power MOSFETPower BJT
ControlVoltage; gate draws no steady currentCurrent; needs large base drive
Switching speedVery fast (majority carriers, no storage time)Slower (storage time ts)
Temperature coefficientRDS(on) rises with T → current sharing, no thermal runawayNegative feedback missing → thermal runaway and second breakdown risk
ON dropIDRDS(on) (small at low voltage)VCE(sat) ≈ 0.2–1 V
Best forHigh frequency, low to medium voltageVery high voltage and current at low frequency
10-mark answer skeleton
  1. Why a vertical structure; labelled cross-section (3).
  2. Key datasheet parameters: VDSS, ID, RDS(on), Qg, SOA (2).
  3. Switching states and the Miller plateau with waveforms (3).
  4. Loss equations and the thermal estimate; comparison with the BJT (2).
Quick check: why must you not drive a power MOSFET only to VGS(th)?

VGS(th) is only where a tiny test current begins to flow. RDS(on) is specified at a much higher gate voltage (often 10 V). Below that the channel is poor and the device dissipates too much power.
