Start here

Six ideas behind everything in this unit

A single transistor stage rarely gives enough gain or the right input and output resistance. Unit 3 is about joining stages and understanding what the joining does.

Idea 1 · Stages load each other

The input resistance of stage 2 is a real resistor hanging on the collector of stage 1. So stage 1’s gain must be calculated with stage 2 connected. Multiplying the unloaded gains of the two stages is the classic wrong answer, and it gives a result that is far too big.

Idea 2 · Gain of each stage = −gm × (its own loaded collector resistance)
A_{v1} = −g_{m1}R_{C1}′,   R_{C1}′ = R_{C1} ∥ r_{o1} ∥ R_{i2}  →  A_{v} = A_{v1}A_{v2}

The “next stage’s Ri” is the only new idea compared with Unit 1. The rest is hybrid-π from Unit 1.

Idea 3 · Ri comes from the first stage, Ro from the last

The source only ever sees the first transistor’s input. The load only ever sees the last transistor’s output. Everything in between changes gain, not what the outside world sees (to first order). That is why a follower is put first when you need high Ri and last when you need low Ro.

Idea 4 · Every capacitor creates a corner frequency

Coupling and bypass capacitors kill the gain at low frequency. Junction capacitances (and the Miller multiplication of Cμ) kill the gain at high frequency. Between the corners is the mid-band, where all the “gain = −gmR′” formulas are valid.

Idea 5 · Cascading narrows the bandwidth

Each added stage adds another pole at each end. The −3 dB point of the combination is reached sooner: fL goes up, fH goes down. More gain costs bandwidth.

Idea 6 · Three coupling methods and two special stages
  • RC coupling: a capacitor between stages. Independent bias, no DC passes.
  • Direct coupling: a wire between stages. DC passes, so bias and drift are shared.
  • Transformer coupling: a transformer. Matches impedances, delivers power.
  • Darlington: two transistors as one, huge current gain. Cascode: CE on CB, high gain with wide bandwidth.
Topic 1

BJT behaviour at low, mid and high frequency

Base of every RC-coupled question, including “derive the mid-band gain”Notes: Section 1–2

1.1 The three regions

Intuition

A capacitor’s reactance is 1/(2πfC). At low frequency this is large, so a series coupling capacitor blocks the signal and a bypass capacitor stops bypassing. At high frequency the reactance is small, so tiny capacitances inside the transistor, which are open circuits at audio, start to short the signal away. In between, the big capacitors are shorts and the tiny ones are opens: a clean region with constant gain.

RegionCoupling / bypass CJunction C (Cπ, Cμ)Model to use
Low (f < fL)Reactance comparable to resistors: they set the cornersOpenMid-band model + the external capacitors
MidShort circuitsOpen circuitsPlain hybrid-π, gain constant
High (f > fH)ShortReactance comparable to resistors: they set the cornersHigh-frequency hybrid-π with Cπ, Cμ

1.2 Mid-band parameters (revision of Unit 1)

g_{m} = [[I_{C}|V_{T}]],   r_{π} = [[β|g_{m}]],   r_{e} ≈ [[1|g_{m}]],   r_{o} ≈ [[V_{A}|I_{C}]]   (V_{T} ≈ 25 mV)
A_{v} ≈ −g_{m}R_{C}′  (emitter bypassed)    A_{v} ≈ −[[g_{m}R_{C}′|1 + g_{m}R_{E}]]  (emitter not bypassed)

where RC′ = RC ∥ ro ∥ RL (or ∥ Ri of the next stage in a cascade).

Worked Problem 1 (notes): mid-band CE parameters and gain

IC = 1.5 mA, β = 120, VA = 90 V, RC = 3.3 kΩ, RL = 10 kΩ, emitter bypassed.

gm = 1.5 mA/25 mV = 60 mS. rπ = 120/0.06 = 2.0 kΩ. re = 1/gm = 16.7 Ω. ro = 90/1.5 mA = 60 kΩ.

RC′ = 3.3k ∥ 10k ∥ 60k = 2.383 kΩ, so Av ≈ −(0.06)(2383) = −143.

1.3 Low-frequency response: three capacitors, three corners

Look at the single CE stage with input coupling C1, emitter bypass CE and output coupling C2. For each capacitor, ask one question: what total resistance does this capacitor see, looking out of its two terminals with all sources turned off? The corner frequency is then 1/(2π × that resistance × C).

  1. C1 (input). Sees the source resistance in series with the amplifier’s input resistance: Ri = R1 ∥ R2 ∥ rπ. So fp1 = 1/[2πC1(rs + Ri)].
  2. C2 (output). Sees the output resistance Ro = RC ∥ ro in series with RL. So fp2 = 1/[2πC2(Ro + RL)].
  3. CE (bypass). Sees RE in parallel with the small resistance looking into the emitter, (rπ + RB)/(β + 1), where RB = rs ∥ R1 ∥ R2. That is only a few tens of ohms, so CE must be hundreds of µF. This is why bypass capacitors are the big electrolytics.
C_{1} ≥ [[1|2πf_{p1}(r_{s}+R_{i})]],   C_{2} ≥ [[1|2πf_{p2}(R_{o}+R_{L})]],   C_{E} ≥ [[1|2πf_{pE}R_{E,seen}]],   R_{E,seen} = R_{E} ∥ [[r_{π}+R_{B}|β+1]]

With several corners, the highest one dominates fL. In a design you place each individual pole below the required fL (or make one intentionally dominant) because the corners add up in the overall response.

Worked Problem 2 (notes): design C1, CE, C2 for 20 Hz poles

rs = 600 Ω, R1 = 47 kΩ, R2 = 10 kΩ, rπ = 2.5 kΩ, β = 100, RE = 1 kΩ, RC = 4.7 kΩ, ro = 100 kΩ, RL = 10 kΩ.

Ri = 47k ∥ 10k ∥ 2.5k = 1.918 kΩ → C1 = 1/[2π(20)(600 + 1918)] = 3.16 µF.

RB = 600 ∥ 47k ∥ 10k = 559 Ω → RE,seen = 1k ∥ (2500+559)/101 = 29.4 Ω → CE = 1/[2π(20)(29.4)] = 271 µF.

Ro = 4.7k ∥ 100k = 4.489 kΩ → C2 = 1/[2π(20)(4489 + 10000)] = 0.549 µF.

Standard values: 4.7 µF, 330 µF, 0.68 µF (round up, which moves the pole lower).

10-mark answer skeleton: “Explain the frequency response of a BJT amplifier”
  1. Gain-vs-frequency curve on a log axis with fL, fH, mid-band gain, −3 dB line and bandwidth shaded (3).
  2. Why the gain falls at low frequency: coupling and bypass capacitor reactance; one sentence per capacitor and the formula for its corner (3).
  3. Why the gain falls at high frequency: Cπ, Cμ and Miller effect (see Topic 2) (3).
  4. BW = fH − fL (1).
Quick check 1: why is CE so much larger than C1?

CE sees only ~30 Ω (the emitter-side resistance), while C1 sees kΩ. For the same corner frequency C ∝ 1/R, so CE must be about 100 times larger.

Quick check 2: the mid-band gain is 40 dB. At what gain are fL and fH?

At 37 dB (3 dB below mid-band), i.e. at 0.707 of the mid-band voltage gain.

Topic 2

High-frequency response, Miller effect and fT

Cascode (Topic 10) is only understandable after this topicNotes: Section 2.3; Worked Problem 3

2.1 The high-frequency hybrid-π model

Intuition

A transistor has two p–n junctions. Each junction stores charge and so behaves like a capacitor. The base–emitter junction (forward biased) gives Cπ (also written Ce), tens of pF. The base–collector junction (reverse biased) gives Cμ (Cc), a few pF. Add the base spreading resistance rbb′ (the resistance of the thin base between the pin and the active region) and the model is complete.

2.2 Why Cμ is the troublemaker: Miller theorem

Cμ sits between the input node (B′) and the output node (C), where the voltage is a large, inverted copy of the input. Miller’s theorem lets you replace it by two grounded capacitors, one at each end.

Derivation (added; the notes give the result)

Current leaving node 1 through Z: I = (v1 − v2)/Z = v1(1 − K)/Z. So the impedance seen at the input is v1/I:

Z_{1} = [[Z|1 − K]]

Current entering node 2 through Z: I = (v2 − v1)/Z with v1 = v2/K, so I = v2(K − 1)/(KZ). Seen from the output:

Z_{2} = [[ZK|K − 1]]

For a capacitor Z = 1/(jωCμ), and with K = Avbc a large negative number:

C_{Mi} = C_{μ}(1 − A_{vbc}) = C_{μ}(1 + |A_{vbc}|)    C_{Mo} = C_{μ}(1 − [[1|A_{vbc}]]) ≈ C_{μ}

Physical meaning. When the base rises by 1 mV, the collector falls by |Av| mV. Across Cμ the voltage changes by (1 + |Av|) mV, so it draws (1 + |Av|) times the charging current. From the input it looks like a capacitor (1 + |Av|) times bigger. A 4 pF capacitor with gain −40 looks like 164 pF.

2.3 Input and output poles

C_{in} = C_{π} + C_{μ}(1 − A_{vbc}),   f_{Hi} = [[1|2πR_{Hi}C_{in}]]    f_{Ho} = [[1|2πR_{Ho}C_{Mo}]]

RHi is the resistance across Cin: rπ in parallel with (rbb′ + rs ∥ R1 ∥ R2). RHo is the resistance at the collector node, RC ∥ ro ∥ RL. The lower of the two poles dominates fH. In almost every high-gain CE stage it is the input pole, because of the Miller multiplication.

Worked Problem 3 (notes): Miller effect and upper cut-off

Cπ = 25 pF, Cμ = 4 pF, Avbc = −40, RHi = 5 kΩ, RHo = 3 kΩ.

Cin = 25 + 4[1 − (−40)] = 189 pF. fHi = 1/[2π(5k)(189p)] = 168 kHz.

CMo = 4(1 − 1/(−40)) = 4.10 pF. fHo = 1/[2π(3k)(4.10p)] ≈ 12.9 MHz.

The input pole is 77 times lower, so the Miller-multiplied input capacitance sets fH.

2.4 The transition frequency fT

fT is the frequency at which the short-circuit current gain |hfe| falls to 1. With the output shorted, the current gain is β/(1 + jωrπ(Cπ + Cμ)), which falls to 1 at

f_{T} = [[g_{m}|2π(C_{π} + C_{μ})]]

It measures how fast the transistor itself is. For gm = 40 mS, Cπ = 25 pF, Cμ = 4 pF: fT ≈ 220 MHz. (The expression above is the notes’ formula; the one-line derivation is added.) A usable amplifier bandwidth is always far below fT because of Miller multiplication.

Exam traps
  • Miller multiplies Cμ by (1 + |Av|) only at the input. At the output it is about Cμ.
  • Avbc is the gain from the internal base B′ to the collector, not from the source. Use −gmRC′.
  • Do not mix the notation: Ce = Cπ and Cc = Cμ. The notes use both.
10-mark answer skeleton: “Explain the high-frequency response of a CE amplifier”
  1. High-frequency hybrid-π figure with rbb′, rπ, Cπ, Cμ, gmvb′e, rce (3).
  2. Statement and derivation of Miller’s theorem (3).
  3. Cin = Cπ + Cμ(1 + gmRC′), CMo ≈ Cμ and the two poles (2).
  4. fH set by the lower pole; fT formula (2).
Quick check 1: gain −100, Cμ = 3 pF. Miller input capacitance?

3 × (1 + 100) = 303 pF.

Quick check 2: why does halving the gain roughly double fH in a high-gain stage?

When |Av| ≫ 1 the Miller term dominates Cin, so Cin ∝ |Av| and fHi ∝ 1/|Av|. That is the gain–bandwidth trade-off (a standard result, not in the notes).

Topic 3

Why multistage? Classification and coupling methods

Notes: Section 3 · Short answer, 4–6 marks

3.1 The need

Intuition

One CE stage gives a gain of perhaps 50 to 150. A microphone gives millivolts and a speaker needs volts. You need 1000 or more. Also, a single stage has one input resistance and one output resistance, and they are rarely what you need: a high-resistance sensor wants a high Ri (use a follower first), and a low-resistance speaker wants a low Ro (use a follower or power stage last). So you chain stages, each doing one job.

  • Gain: the total is the product of the stage gains (or the sum, in dB).
  • Impedance matching: choose each stage’s Ri and Ro for the neighbours.
  • Bandwidth and drive: a wideband stage, a power stage and so on.

3.2 Classification

Multistage amplifiers are classified in three ways. In an exam, name all three.

  • By coupling: RC, direct, transformer.
  • By signal type: small-signal voltage amplifier stages or large-signal (power) output stages.
  • By configuration of the stages: CE–CE, CE–CC, CC–CE, CE–CB (cascode), Darlington.

3.3 The three coupling methods

MethodMain advantageMain limitationTypical use
RC couplingSimple, cheap, independent DC bias of each stageLow-frequency roll-off; interstage loading; cannot amplify DCGeneral voltage amplification, audio
Direct couplingNo coupling capacitor; response extends to DCDC bias and drift of each stage affect the nextDC and very-low-frequency amplifiers, ICs
Transformer couplingImpedance matching, DC isolation, power transferBulky, expensive; bandwidth limited by transformer non-idealitiesPower/output stages
10-mark answer skeleton: “Need and types of multistage amplifiers”
  1. Need: gain, impedance matching, bandwidth, drive (2).
  2. Block diagram of an n-stage cascade with source and load (2).
  3. Classification (2).
  4. Comparison table of the three couplings with figures (4).
Quick check: which coupling can amplify a DC signal?

Only direct coupling, and only if the external input/output coupling capacitors are also left out.

Topic 4

General parameter evaluation: loading, Ri, Ro, Av, Ai, bandwidth

The method behind every numerical in this unitNotes: Section 4; Worked Problems 4 and 5

4.1 Interstage loading

  1. Work from the load backwards for the gains. Stage 2 has the real load RL, so find Av2 first (RC2′ = RC2 ∥ ro2 ∥ RL).
  2. Find Ri2. It is the load that stage 1 sees.
  3. Then stage 1: RC1′ = RC1 ∥ ro1 ∥ Ri2 and Av1 = −gm1RC1′.
  4. Multiply: Av = Av1Av2. The sign gives the phase: two inverting stages give a positive (in-phase) result.
A_{v} = A_{v1}A_{v2}…,   A_{vs} = A_{v}[[R_{i}|R_{s}+R_{i}]],   A_{i} = [[i_{o}|i_{i}]] = A_{v}[[R_{i}|R_{L}]]

Av is the gain from the amplifier’s own input terminal. Avs is from the source and includes the input divider. The current-gain formula is valid when Av is referenced to the input terminal (vo/vi); if it is referenced to the source, first include Ri/(Rs + Ri).

Why Ai = AvRi/RL: io = vo/RL and ii = vi/Ri, so the ratio is (vo/vi)(Ri/RL).

Ri is mainly the first stage’s input resistance. Ro is mainly the last stage’s output resistance (RC ∥ ro for a CE stage; about 1/gm-ish for a follower).

Worked Problem 4 (notes): two-stage CE with loading

gm1 = 40 mS, RC1 = 4.7 kΩ, ro1 = 80 kΩ, Ri2 = 3 kΩ. gm2 = 35 mS, RC2 = 3.3 kΩ, ro2 = 60 kΩ, RL = 10 kΩ. Ri = 4 kΩ, Rs = 1 kΩ.

RC1′ = 4.7k ∥ 80k ∥ 3k = 1.790 kΩ → Av1 = −(0.04)(1790) = −71.6.

RC2′ = 3.3k ∥ 60k ∥ 10k = 2.383 kΩ → Av2 = −(0.035)(2383) = −83.4.

Av = (−71.6)(−83.4) = +5.97 × 10³. Avs = 5972 × 4/(1 + 4) = 4.78 × 10³. Ro ≈ 3.3k ∥ 60k = 3.13 kΩ. Ai = 5972 × 4k/10k = 2.39 × 10³.

Without loading, stage 1 would have claimed −(0.04)(4.7k ∥ 80k) = −178 instead of −71.6. That is the cost of ignoring Ri2.

4.2 Bandwidth of a cascade

Each first-order stage contributes its own low-frequency and high-frequency pole. For n identical stages, the overall response at frequency f (normalised to mid-band) is the single-stage response raised to the power n. Set it equal to 1/√2 and solve.

Derivation (the notes give the result)

One stage, upper pole: |A|/Amid = [1 + (f/fH,s)²]−1/2. For n stages: [1 + (f/fH,s)²]−n/2. At the overall −3 dB point this equals 2−1/2:

[1 + (f_{H}/f_{H,s})²]^{n} = 2  ⟹  (f_{H}/f_{H,s})² = 2^{1/n} − 1

and by the same steps for the lower pole (fL,s/fL)² = 21/n − 1.

f_{H} = f_{H,s}√(2^{1/n} − 1),    f_{L} = [[f_{L,s}|√(2^{1/n} − 1)]]

The shrinkage factor √(21/n − 1) is 1 for n = 1, 0.644 for n = 2, 0.510 for n = 3, 0.435 for n = 4. So two stages keep only 64% of the single-stage fH, and fL rises by 1/0.644 = 1.55 times.

Worked Problem 5 (notes): two identical stages

fL,s = 50 Hz, fH,s = 100 kHz.

fL = 50/√(21/2 − 1) = 77.7 Hz. fH = 100 kHz × √(21/2 − 1) = 64.4 kHz. BW ≈ 64.4 kHz − 0.078 kHz = 64.3 kHz.

The exact formulas are for identical first-order stages. For quick qualitative reasoning with unequal stages, the stage with the highest fL and the stage with the lowest fH dominate (the “dominant-stage idea”).

10-mark answer skeleton: “Evaluate the parameters of a multistage amplifier”
  1. Block diagram with Rs, stage 1, stage 2, RL (2).
  2. Loading: RC1′ = RC1 ∥ ro1 ∥ Ri2 (2).
  3. Av, Avs, Ai with formulas (3).
  4. Ri from first stage, Ro from last stage (1).
  5. Bandwidth shrinkage with the n-stage formulas (2).
Quick check 1: three identical stages, each Av = 20 dB, fH,s = 200 kHz. Gain and fH?

60 dB overall. fH = 200 kHz × √(21/3 − 1) = 200 × 0.510 = 102 kHz.

Quick check 2: why not multiply the unloaded gains?

Because stage 1 drives Ri2 and its gain is then −gm1(RC1 ∥ ro1 ∥ Ri2), which is smaller. Using unloaded gains over-estimates the total.

Topic 5

Choice of transistor configuration in a cascade

Notes: Section 5 · 4–5 mark question
Intuition

Each configuration has a personality. CE gives gain. CC gives a buffer (voltage gain just under 1, high Ri, low Ro). CB has a low Ri and a good high-frequency response. Pick each stage for the job it has to do. The stages in a cascade need not all be the same.

ConfigurationVoltage gainRiRoWhy chosen
CEHigh, invertingModerateModerate to highMain voltage-gain stage
CC (emitter follower)Near unity, no inversionHighLowInput/output buffer, load driver
CBHigh, no inversionLowHighHigh-frequency stage; upper transistor of a cascode
DarlingtonNear unity as followerVery highLowVery high current gain and high Ri
Cascode (CE–CB)HighModerateHighHigh gain with improved high-frequency response

Typical cascades

  • CE → CE: high overall voltage gain; the standard RC-coupled voltage amplifier.
  • CE → CC: voltage gain, then a low-Ro buffer so that a low-resistance load does not collapse the gain.
  • CC → CE: a high-Ri front end for a weak, high-resistance source, then voltage gain.
  • CE → CB: the cascode (Topic 10) for high gain and wide bandwidth.
  • Darlington → CE or Darlington output stage: when the source cannot supply much input current.
Exam use

When a question gives a source resistance and a load resistance and asks “which configuration for each stage”, answer with the table: high-Rs source → CC/Darlington first; small RL → CC/power stage last; gain in the middle with CE.

Quick check: a 100 kΩ sensor drives an 8 Ω speaker. Sketch the chain.

CC/Darlington (high Ri, not loading the sensor) → CE (voltage gain) → CC or power stage (low Ro to drive 8 Ω). In practice the speaker stage is transformer-coupled or an emitter-follower power stage.

Topic 6

RC-coupled multistage amplifier

Asked: Summer 2019 Q1 (a) “Derive mid-band voltage gain of RC-coupled amplifier” (6) and Q1 (b) numerical (4)Notes: Section 6; Worked Problems 6 and 7

6.1 The circuit and what each part does

  • Stage 1 amplifies the input voltage. C3 carries the AC to stage 2 but blocks stage 1’s DC collector voltage, so each stage is biased independently.
  • Stage 2 gives more gain. C5 passes the amplified AC to the load and blocks DC.
  • C2, C4 bypass the emitter resistors so the AC gain is not reduced by emitter degeneration.

6.2 Derivation of the mid-band gain

At mid-band every external capacitor is a short and every junction capacitor is open. So the emitters of both transistors are at AC ground, and Figure 9 reduces to two hybrid-π CE stages with the divider resistors in parallel.

  1. Input resistances. Ri1 = R1 ∥ R2 ∥ rπ1 and Ri2 = R5 ∥ R6 ∥ rπ2.
  2. Loaded collector resistances. RC1′ = R3 ∥ ro1 ∥ Ri2 (stage 1 is loaded by stage 2) and RC2′ = R7 ∥ ro2 ∥ RL.
  3. Stage gains. Each transistor gives ic = gmvπ, and the current flows in the collector load. So Av1 = −gm1RC1′ and Av2 = −gm2RC2′.
  4. Overall. vo/vb1 = Av1Av2. From the source: vo/vs = [Ri1/(rs + Ri1)]Av1Av2.
  5. Terminal resistances (first order). Ri = Ri1 and Ro ≈ R7 ∥ ro2.
A_{v} = (−g_{m1}R_{C1}′)(−g_{m2}R_{C2}′),   [[v_{o}|v_{s}]] = [[R_{i1}|r_{s}+R_{i1}]]A_{v1}A_{v2}
Worked Problem 6 (notes): mid-band analysis

Both transistors: IC = 1 mA, β = 100, gm = 40 mS, rπ = 2.5 kΩ, ro = 100 kΩ. R1 = R5 = 47 kΩ, R2 = R6 = 10 kΩ, R3 = R7 = 4.7 kΩ, rs = 600 Ω, RL = 10 kΩ.

Ri1 = Ri2 = 47k ∥ 10k ∥ 2.5k = 1.918 kΩ.

RC1′ = 4.7k ∥ 100k ∥ 1.918k = 1.344 kΩ → Av1 = −53.8. RC2′ = 4.7k ∥ 100k ∥ 10k = 3.098 kΩ → Av2 = −123.9.

Av = +6.66 × 10³. vo/vs = 6662 × 1.918/(0.6 + 1.918) = 5.08 × 10³. Ro ≈ 4.7k ∥ 100k = 4.49 kΩ.

The notes warn that this is a small-signal result: signal swing, nonlinearity and bias headroom limit the usable gain in practice.

6.3 Choosing all five capacitors

The notes extend the single-stage rule to each capacitor of Figure 9:

C_{1} ≥ [[1|2πf_{p1}(r_{s}+R_{i1})]],   C_{3} ≥ [[1|2πf_{p3}(R_{o1}+R_{i2})]],   C_{5} ≥ [[1|2πf_{p5}(R_{o2}+R_{L})]]
C_{2} ≥ [[1|2πf_{p2}R_{E1,seen}]],   R_{E1,seen} = R_{4} ∥ [[r_{π1}+R_{B1}|β_{1}+1]],   R_{B1} = r_{s} ∥ R_{1} ∥ R_{2}
C_{4} ≥ [[1|2πf_{p4}R_{E2,seen}]],   R_{E2,seen} = R_{8} ∥ [[r_{π2}+R_{B2}|β_{2}+1]],   R_{B2} = R_{5} ∥ R_{6} ∥ R_{o1}

Here Ro1 ≈ R3 ∥ ro1 and Ro2 ≈ R7 ∥ ro2. The base resistance of stage 2 seen from its emitter is the previous stage’s output resistance in parallel with the divider. For a specified overall fL, assign each individual pole a little below it.

Worked Problem 7 (notes): all five capacitors for 10 Hz poles

Same amplifier as Problem 6, R4 = R8 = 1 kΩ.

C1 = 1/[2π(10)(0.6k + 1.918k)] = 6.32 µF. C3 = 1/[2π(10)(4.489k + 1.918k)] = 2.48 µF. C5 = 1/[2π(10)(4.489k + 10k)] = 1.10 µF.

RE1,seen = 29.4 Ω → C2 = 541 µF. RB2 = 47k ∥ 10k ∥ 4.489k = 2.907 kΩ, RE2,seen = 1k ∥ (2.5k + 2.907k)/101 = 50.8 Ω → C4 = 313 µF.

Standard choices: C1 = 10 µF, C3 = 3.3 µF, C5 = 1.5 µF, C2 = 560 µF, C4 = 330 µF. A larger standard value moves the pole lower.

6.4 Design procedure

  1. Decide the total gain and split it between the stages.
  2. Choose IC of each stage and put VCE near mid-swing.
  3. Choose RC and RE to establish the Q-points.
  4. Choose the base divider of each stage.
  5. Check interstage loading and revise the gain.
  6. Choose coupling and bypass capacitors for the required fL.
  7. Check the transistor’s high-frequency capability (fT, Miller effect) if a wide fH is needed.
Summer 2019 numerical: how to attack it with h-parameters

The paper’s Q1 OR (b) gives hfe = 50, hie = 1200 Ω, gm = 40 mS, R1 = 110 kΩ, R2 = 10 kΩ. A route that fits the notes: Rb = R1 ∥ R2 = 9.17 kΩ; rb′e = hfe/gm = 50/0.04 = 1.25 kΩ; Ri = Rb ∥ hie = 9.17k ∥ 1.2k = 1.06 kΩ; Ro = RC ∥ 1/hoe (the hoe exponent and RC value are unreadable in my scan, so substitute them from your paper).

10-mark answer skeleton: “Derive the mid-band voltage gain of an RC-coupled amplifier”
  1. Circuit of the two-stage amplifier with all components named (2).
  2. Mid-band assumptions: coupling and bypass capacitors short, junction capacitors open; mid-band hybrid-π equivalent (2).
  3. Ri2, RC1′, Av1 with loading; then RC2′, Av2 (3).
  4. Av = Av1Av2 and Avs with the input divider (1).
  5. Ri, Ro and one comment on the frequency response (2).
Quick check 1: why is each stage biased independently?

C3 blocks DC, so stage 1’s collector voltage does not change stage 2’s base bias. Each stage has its own divider and RE.

Quick check 2: two CE stages: phase between vo and vb1?

Each inverts 180°, so the product is positive: in phase.

Topic 7

Direct-coupled amplifier

Asked: Summer 2019 Q1 OR (a) “How is the direct-coupled amplifier developed?” (6)Notes: Section 7; Worked Problem 8

7.1 How it develops from the RC-coupled amplifier

Intuition (use this as the “development” story)
  1. Start with the two-stage RC-coupled amplifier. The interstage capacitor C3 blocks DC, so the lower cut-off is non-zero, and DC or very slow signals cannot pass.
  2. Remove C3 and the stage-2 divider (R5, R6 in Figure 9) and connect collector of Q1 directly to base of Q2. Now the collector DC level of Q1 is the base bias of Q2.
  3. Q2’s base current is drawn through Q1’s collector resistor, so it must be included in the R3 current.
  4. Result: the interstage path passes both AC and DC. The price is that bias and temperature drift now propagate from stage to stage.

7.2 DC design equations

V_{B1} ≈ V_{CC}[[R_{2}|R_{1}+R_{2}]],   V_{E1} = V_{B1} − V_{BE1},   I_{E1} ≈ [[V_{E1}|R_{4}]],   I_{C1} ≈ α_{1}I_{E1}
V_{B2} = V_{C1} = V_{CC} − R_{3}(I_{C1} + I_{B2}),   V_{E2} = V_{B2} − V_{BE2},   I_{E2} ≈ [[V_{E2}|R_{6}]],   I_{B2} ≈ [[I_{E2}|β_{2}+1]]
V_{C2} = V_{CC} − I_{C2}R_{5}

Because IB2 depends on VB2, which depends on IB2, solve it once as one linear equation. A first pass may neglect IB2 (VC1 ≈ VCC − IC1R3), then correct it.

7.3 Mid-band small-signal analysis

With C2, C3 bypassing R4, R6, and no divider on Q2, rπ2 alone loads the collector of Q1:

R_{C1}′ = R_{3} ∥ r_{o1} ∥ r_{π2},   R_{C2}′ = R_{5} ∥ r_{o2} ∥ R_{L},   A_{v} ≈ (−g_{m1}R_{C1}′)(−g_{m2}R_{C2}′)
Worked Problem 8 (notes): DC analysis of Figure 10

VCC = 12 V, R1 = 82 kΩ, R2 = 18 kΩ, R3 = 3.3 kΩ, R4 = 1 kΩ, R5 = 680 Ω, R6 = 1.5 kΩ, β1 = β2 = 100.

VB1 ≈ 12 × 18/100 = 2.16 V → VE1 = 1.46 V → IC1 ≈ (100/101)(1.46 mA) = 1.446 mA.

VB2 = 12 − 3.3k[IC1 + (VB2 − 0.7)/(1.5k × 101)] ≈ 7.09 V, VE2 ≈ 6.39 V → IE2 ≈ 4.26 mA, IC2 ≈ 4.22 mA.

VC2 = 12 − (4.22 mA)(680) = 9.13 V, VCE2 ≈ 2.74 V. Both transistors keep VCE headroom (VCE1 = VB2 − VE1 ≈ 5.6 V).

Drift and the fL statement (the notes are careful here)
  • Drift: a small change in VBE or IC in stage 1 is amplified by stage 2 and looks like a signal. Treat the circuit as one DC system.
  • Lower cut-off: the direct connection adds no interstage pole. But the circuit in Figure 10 still has C1, C2, C3, C4, so fL is not zero. fL = 0 only for a fully DC-coupled amplifier with the external coupling capacitors also removed.
6–10 mark answer skeleton: “Direct-coupled amplifier”
  1. Development from the RC-coupled amplifier (what is removed, what is joined) (2).
  2. Circuit (Figure 10) (2).
  3. DC bias equations including IB2 in R3 (2).
  4. Mid-band gain with rπ2 as the load of stage 1 (2).
  5. Merits: response to DC, fewer parts. Demerits: drift, bias interdependence (2).
Quick check: why must IB2 be included in the R3 current?

Q2’s base is connected straight to Q1’s collector node, so Q2’s base current is drawn from the supply through R3, together with IC1.

Topic 8

Transformer-coupled amplifier

Asked: Summer 2019 Q3 (b) reflected load for 150 Ω and a 5 : 2 transformerNotes: Section 8; Worked Problem 9

8.1 Why a transformer?

Intuition

A transistor delivers its best power into a load of some kΩ (a few hundred ohms to a few kΩ for a typical power stage). A loudspeaker is 4–16 Ω. Connect them directly and almost no power transfers. A transformer is a “gearbox” for impedance: it reflects the 8 Ω speaker to look like a few kΩ at the collector. It also gives DC isolation between the collector circuit and the load.

8.2 Reflected load and turns ratio

For an ideal transformer with n = N1/N2: voltages scale as V1/V2 = n, currents inversely, I2/I1 = n.

Derivation

The primary sees R′ = V1/I1. Substitute V1 = nV2 and I1 = I2/n:

R_{L}′ = [[V_{1}|I_{1}]] = [[nV_{2}|I_{2}/n]] = n²[[V_{2}|I_{2}]] = n²R_{L}
R_{L}′ = n²R_{L} = (N_{1}/N_{2})²R_{L},    n = √[[R_{L}′|R_{L}]]

With the emitter at AC ground and an ideal transformer in mid-band, the collector load is RC′ ≈ RL′ ∥ ro, so

A_{v,p} ≈ −g_{m}R_{C}′ (at the primary),    A_{v,sec} ≈ [[A_{v,p}|n]] (at the secondary)

The secondary voltage is smaller than the primary by n when n > 1 (a step-down). The power transfer, not the voltage, is the goal.

8.3 Frequency limits of the transformer

  • Low frequency: the magnetizing inductance Lm sits in parallel with the reflected load and shunts the signal. First-order corner: fL ≈ RL′/(2πLm).
  • High frequency: leakage inductance and winding capacitance limit the response.
Worked Problem 9 (notes)

An 8 Ω load is to appear as 2 kΩ at the collector. Required fL = 50 Hz, gm = 40 mS, ro = 100 kΩ.

n = √(2000/8) = 15.8 ≈ 16 : 1. Lm = 2000/(2π × 50) = 6.37 H. RC′ = 2k ∥ 100k = 1.961 kΩ, Av,p = −(0.04)(1961) = −78.4, Av,sec = −78.4/15.8 = −4.96.

A practical transformer would have a turns ratio near 16 : 1 and at least about 6.4 H of magnetizing inductance.

Summer 2019 question

RL = 150 Ω, turns ratio 5 : 2, so n = 2.5 and RL′ = (2.5)²(150) = 6.25 × 150 = 937.5 Ω (reflected to the primary).

Transformer coupling is most attractive when power transfer and impedance matching matter more than size, cost or very wide bandwidth.

10-mark answer skeleton: “Transformer-coupled amplifier”
  1. Circuit with the output transformer (2).
  2. Need: impedance matching, DC isolation, power transfer (2).
  3. Derivation RL′ = n²RL (2).
  4. Gain, turns ratio selection (2).
  5. Frequency response curve with Lm and leakage/capacitance limits; merits and demerits (2).
Quick check: what does a 10 : 1 transformer do to a 16 Ω load?

RL′ = 10² × 16 = 1.6 kΩ.

Topic 9

Darlington amplifier

Notes: Section 9; Worked Problem 10
Intuition

Take two transistors and let the emitter of the first drive the base of the second. The tiny base current of Q2 is multiplied by β2 in its emitter, and that whole emitter current becomes the base current of Q1, which multiplies it again by β1. Total current gain is roughly β1β2. With β = 100 each, one base electron controls 10 000 collector electrons.

9.1 Compound current gain

I_{B1} = I_{E2} = (β_{2}+1)I_{B2},    I_{E1} = (β_{1}+1)I_{B1} = (β_{1}+1)(β_{2}+1)I_{B2}
β_{D} ≡ [[I_{C1}+I_{C2}|I_{B2}]] = β_{1}β_{2} + β_{1} + β_{2} ≈ β_{1}β_{2}

The product approximation is adequate when both β are large. The base–emitter voltage of the pair is about two diode drops (1.2–1.4 V for silicon), so the output emitter sits two VBE below the input base:

V_{E1} ≈ V_{B2} − V_{BE2} − V_{BE1},    I_{E1,Q} ≈ [[V_{E1}|R_{3}]]

9.2 Input resistance and gain

At mid-band C2 is a short, so the emitter of Q1 sees RE′ = R3 ∥ RL. Work backwards from the output along the signal path:

  1. Into the base of Q1: Rin,B1 = rπ1 + (β1 + 1)RE′. This is the load on the emitter of Q2.
  2. Into the base of Q2: Rin,B2 = rπ2 + (β2 + 1)Rin,B1. A follower multiplies whatever is at its emitter by (β + 1).
  3. With the bias divider in parallel: Ri = (R1 ∥ R2) ∥ Rin,B2.
A_{v1} ≈ [[(β_{1}+1)R_{E}′|r_{π1}+(β_{1}+1)R_{E}′]],   A_{v2} ≈ [[(β_{2}+1)R_{in,B1}|r_{π2}+(β_{2}+1)R_{in,B1}]],   A_{v} = A_{v1}A_{v2} ≈ 1⁻
Worked Problem 10 (notes)

VCC = 12 V, R1 = 100 kΩ, R2 = 33 kΩ, R3 = 470 Ω, RL = 1 kΩ, β2 = 80 (input transistor), β1 = 100 (output transistor). rπ1 ≈ 0.752 kΩ, rπ2 ≈ 60.9 kΩ.

VB2 ≈ 12 × 33/133 = 2.98 V → VE1 ≈ 2.98 − 1.40 = 1.58 V → IE1 ≈ 1.58/470 = 3.36 mA.

RE′ = 470 ∥ 1k = 319.7 Ω. Rin,B1 = 0.752k + 101(319.7) = 33.0 kΩ. Rin,B2 = 60.9k + 81(33.0k) = 2.74 MΩ.

Ri = (100k ∥ 33k) ∥ 2.74M = 24.6 kΩ. Av ≈ (0.977)(0.978) = 0.955.

The intrinsic input resistance is megohms, but the bias divider caps the actual Ri at 24.6 kΩ. That is the classic Darlington design lesson: bias resistors waste the high Ri, so bootstrap or use a large R1∥R2 (standard remark, not in the notes).

  • Very high current gain and high input resistance; low output resistance; voltage gain just below 1.
  • Needs about 2VBE of drive; slower than a single transistor (from the comparison table in the notes).
  • Check the power in Q1: it carries almost all the load current.
10-mark answer skeleton: “Darlington amplifier”
  1. Circuit (2).
  2. Derivation of βD = β1β2 + β1 + β2 (3).
  3. Ri by successive reflection, Av ≈ 1 (3).
  4. Features and uses; VBE drop of ≈ 1.2–1.4 V (2).
Quick check: β1 = β2 = 50. Exact βD?

50 × 50 + 50 + 50 = 2600 (the approximation gives 2500, only 4% less).

Topic 10

Cascode amplifier

Asked: Summer 2019 Q1 (b) “What is a cascode amplifier?” (4)Notes: Section 10; Worked Problem 11
Intuition

The problem with a CE stage is Miller: its collector swings a lot, and that big swing multiplies Cμ at the input. The cure: let the collector of the input transistor not move. Put a second transistor in common-base on top. Its emitter is a very low impedance (1/gm), so Q1’s collector is almost a short to AC ground and barely swings. Q1 still turns input voltage into current (gm1vπ), Q2 passes that current to the collector load R4, and the big voltage swing happens up at Q2’s collector, away from the input.

Important AC condition (the notes stress this)

For Q2 to be a true common-base transistor its base must be at AC ground: bypass the Q2 base node with a large capacitor to ground (or hold it with a low-impedance bias source). All the gain, output-resistance and Miller expressions below assume this. If the Q2 base moves with the signal, the simple cascode equations no longer apply.

10.1 DC bias

The series divider R1–R2–R3 sets both base voltages (neglecting base current for a first pass):

V_{B1} ≈ V_{CC}[[R_{3}|R_{1}+R_{2}+R_{3}]],   V_{B2} ≈ V_{CC}[[R_{2}+R_{3}|R_{1}+R_{2}+R_{3}]],   I ≈ I_{C1} ≈ I_{C2} ≈ [[V_{B1}−V_{BE1}|R_{5}]]
V_{C1} = V_{E2} ≈ V_{B2} − V_{BE2},   V_{CE1} ≈ V_{C1} − V_{E1},   V_{C2} = V_{CC} − IR_{4},   V_{CE2} = V_{C2} − V_{E2}

Both VCE values must stay in the forward-active region over the whole swing. Two stacked transistors need more supply headroom than one CE stage.

10.2 Gain, output resistance and Miller effect

A_{v} ≈ −g_{m1}R_{C}′,   R_{C}′ = R_{4} ∥ R_{L} ∥ R_{out,casc}
R_{out,casc} ≈ r_{o1} + r_{o2} + g_{m2}r_{o1}r_{o2} ≈ g_{m}r_{o}²   (matched devices)
Why Rout is so large (added reasoning)

Look into the collector of Q2. A change vx there makes a small change in Q2’s emitter voltage. Because Q1’s collector current is held constant by its fixed base voltage, that emitter voltage change is only ixro1. Q2’s own gm2 then amplifies the effect of ro1, so ro1 appears multiplied by (1 + gm2ro2) in series with ro2: Rout ≈ ro2 + ro1(1 + gm2ro2), the notes’ result. The practical effect: the transistor behaves almost like an ideal current source, so RC′ is set by R4 and RL.

A_{vb1} ≡ [[v_{c1}|v_{π1}]] ≈ −[[g_{m1}|g_{m2}]] ≈ −1,    C_{in} ≈ C_{π1} + 2C_{μ1} + C_{stray}

The gain from Q1’s base-emitter voltage to Q1’s collector is only about −1. So Miller multiplies Cμ1 by 1 − (−1) = 2, not by 1 + |Av| ≈ 100.

Worked Problem 11 (notes): bias, gain and Miller reduction

VCC = 12 V, R1 = 47 kΩ, R2 = R3 = 22 kΩ, R4 = 3.3 kΩ, R5 = 2.2 kΩ, RL = 10 kΩ. VBE = 0.7 V, gm1 = gm2 = 40 mS, ro = 100 kΩ, Cπ1 = 25 pF, Cμ1 = 4 pF, Cstray = 3 pF.

VB1 = 12 × 22/91 = 2.90 V, VB2 = 12 × 44/91 = 5.80 V, VE1 = 2.20 V → I = 2.20/2.2k = 1.00 mA.

VC1 = VE2 = 5.80 − 0.70 = 5.10 V → VCE1 = 2.90 V. VC2 = 12 − 3.3 = 8.70 V → VCE2 = 3.60 V.

Rout,casc ≈ 100k + 100k + (0.04)(100k)² ≈ 400 MΩ. RC′ = 3.3k ∥ 10k ∥ 400M = 2.48 kΩ → Av ≈ −(0.04)(2480) = −99.2.

Cin,casc ≈ 25 + 2(4) + 3 = 36 pF. For a plain CE stage with the same gain, Cin,CE ≈ 25 + 4(1 + 99.2) + 3 ≈ 429 pF. Same gain, about 12 times less input capacitance, so fH improves by roughly the same factor.

10.3 Design steps and when to choose a cascode

  1. Choose IC from the gain, noise and power considerations; bias Q1 for it.
  2. Choose VB2 so that Q1’s collector sits at a nearly constant voltage with enough VCE1.
  3. Choose R4 for the gain and swing.
  4. Check both transistors stay in forward-active over the swing, and check the supply headroom.

CE or cascode? Choose a plain CE stage when simplicity and gain are the aims. Choose a cascode when the same gain is needed with a better high-frequency response and less input–output feedback.

  • High voltage gain like a good CE stage; very high output resistance; reduced Miller effect; better isolation between input and output; more supply voltage and more components needed.
4-mark and 10-mark answer skeletons: “What is a cascode amplifier?”
  1. 4 marks: a CE transistor driving a CB transistor (draw it); the CB device keeps the CE collector nearly fixed, so Miller multiplication of Cμ is about 2 instead of (1 + |Av|); result: high gain and wide bandwidth, with a high output resistance.
  2. 10 marks, add: circuit with bias ladder and AC-grounded Q2 base (2), DC equations (2), Av ≈ −gm1RC′ and Rout ≈ gmro² (3), Miller reduction with numbers (2), merits and demerits (1).
Quick check 1: gain of the Q1 stage alone in a matched cascode?

About −gm1/gm2 = −1, because Q2’s emitter resistance 1/gm2 is the load.

Quick check 2: why the extra supply voltage?

Two transistors are stacked in series, so VCC must cover two VCE values, the emitter resistor drop and the collector resistor drop.

Topic 11

Comparison and design workflow

Notes: Sections 11 and 13 · 4–6 mark “compare” questions
Circuit / methodMain strengthMain weaknessBest suited for
RC-coupled CE cascadeHigh voltage gain with simple, independent biasingLow-frequency roll-off from capacitors; interstage loadingGeneral voltage amplification
Direct coupledResponse extends to DCBias drift and stage-to-stage DC dependenceDC and very-low-frequency signals
Transformer coupledImpedance matching and power transferSize, cost and limited bandwidthPower/output stage
DarlingtonVery high current gain and input resistanceAbout two VBE drops; slower than a single transistorBuffer and current driver
CascodeHigh gain with wide bandwidth and low Miller effectMore components and supply headroomWideband / high-frequency voltage amplifier

Seven-step design workflow

  1. Start from the required gain, input resistance, output resistance, bandwidth and signal swing.
  2. Select stage configurations by the function of each stage.
  3. Complete the DC bias design first.
  4. Calculate the loaded mid-band gains and verify the total gain.
  5. Choose coupling and bypass components and check the low-frequency response.
  6. Check high-frequency limits, especially Miller effect in high-gain CE stages.
  7. Check signal swing, power dissipation, tolerances and temperature stability.
Final design perspective (notes)

A good multistage amplifier is not simply a collection of high-gain stages. Source loading, interstage loading, output drive, DC bias, frequency response and signal swing must all be satisfactory at the same time.

Start Here

The core intuition of feedback

Intuition 1 · The Fundamental Trade-Off

In open-loop amplifiers, voltage gain depends heavily on transistor parameters like hfe and gm, which vary wildly (up to 300%) with temperature and batch manufacturing. Negative feedback introduces a fraction β of the output back to oppose the input signal. We sacrifice raw open-loop gain A to gain rock-solid stability, wider bandwidth, and reduced distortion. When loop gain Aβ >> 1, closed-loop gain becomes Af ≈ 1/β, determined entirely by passive resistors!

Intuition 2 · The Desensitivity Factor (1 + Aβ)

Every major benefit of negative feedback is scaled by the amount of feedback (1 + Aβ) (also called the return difference or desensitivity factor D):

  • Gain decreases by (1 + Aβ): Af = A / (1 + Aβ)
  • Gain instability decreases by (1 + Aβ): (dAf/Af) = (1 / (1 + Aβ)) · (dA/A)
  • Bandwidth multiplies by (1 + Aβ): BWf = BW · (1 + Aβ)
  • Harmonic distortion divides by (1 + Aβ): Df = D / (1 + Aβ)
Intuition 3 · The Golden Rule of Impedances

Memorize this foolproof two-part rule for input and output impedance under feedback:

  • Input Mixing: Series connection at input always increases input impedance by (1 + Aβ): Zif = Zi(1 + Aβ). Shunt (parallel) connection at input always decreases input impedance: Zif = Zi / (1 + Aβ).
  • Output Sampling: Voltage sampling (parallel connection across output) always decreases output impedance: Zof = Zo / (1 + Aβ). Current sampling (series connection in output branch) always increases output impedance: Zof = Zo(1 + Aβ).
Topic 1

The four basic amplifier types

Summer 2026 Q4 (10 Marks)Two-Port RepresentationsIdeal Port Resistances
Intuition

An electrical signal can be represented as a voltage or current source, and a load can be controlled by voltage or current. This yields exactly four basic amplifier classes. An ideal amplifier never loads its source or its load.

Amplifier TypeInput Signal (Xi)Output Signal (Xo)Gain ParameterIdeal RinIdeal RoutPractical Goal
Voltage AmplifierVoltage (Vi)Voltage (Vo)Av = Vo/Vi (dimensionless)∞ (open)0 (short)Sense voltage without drawing current; drive load without voltage drop
Current AmplifierCurrent (Ii)Current (Io)Ai = Io/Ii (dimensionless)0 (short)∞ (open)Absorb all source current; act as an ideal current source for load
Transconductance AmplifierVoltage (Vi)Current (Io)Gm = Io/Vi (Siemens, S)∞ (open)∞ (open)Convert input voltage into proportional output current (e.g. FET, OTA)
Transresistance AmplifierCurrent (Ii)Voltage (Vo)Rm = Vo/Ii (Ohms, Ω)0 (short)0 (short)Convert input current into proportional output voltage (e.g. photodiode TIA)
10-mark answer skeleton (Summer 2026 Q4)
  1. Define all four basic amplifiers by identifying input signal variable and output controlled variable (2).
  2. Draw the Thévenin / Norton equivalent circuit representations for Current Amplifier and Transconductance Amplifier (4).
  3. Derive why ideal Rin and Rout values take their infinite/zero values based on voltage/current division (2).
  4. State the practical units of transfer parameters (Av, Ai, Gm in Siemens, Rm in Ohms) (2).
Quick check: Why does an ideal transconductance amplifier require both infinite input and output resistance?

Infinite Rin ensures the amplifier samples the input voltage without drawing any current from the source (no loading). Infinite Rout ensures that all generated output current gmVi flows entirely into the external load regardless of load resistance value.

Topic 2

Feedback concept & transfer gain derivation

Loop Transfer FunctionBarkhausen & Stability
Intuition

Feedback is the process of taking a sample of the output signal and feeding it back into the input. If the returned signal adds to the input, it is positive feedback (amplification grows until oscillation or saturation). If it subtracts from the input, it is negative feedback (self-correcting control loop).

2.1 Mathematical Derivation of Closed-Loop Gain Af

Let:

  • Xs = externally applied input signal (voltage or current)
  • Xf = feedback signal returned by the feedback network
  • Xi = effective input signal applied to the basic amplifier
  • Xo = output signal
  • A = open-loop gain = Xo / Xi
  • β = feedback factor (transfer ratio) = Xf / Xo

At the input comparator (mixer), for negative feedback:

X_{i} = X_{s} − X_{f} = X_{s} − β X_{o}

The basic amplifier amplifies Xi:

X_{o} = A X_{i} = A (X_{s} − β X_{o}) = A X_{s} − A β X_{o}

Rearranging terms to collect Xo on the left side:

X_{o} (1 + A β) = A X_{s}

Dividing by Xs gives the celebrated transfer gain with feedback formula:

A_{f} = [[X_{o}|X_{s}]] = [[A|1 + A β]]

Key quantities:

  • Loop Gain (Return Ratio): T = Aβ
  • Amount of Feedback (Desensitivity Factor): D = 1 + Aβ
  • High Loop Gain Limit: When |Aβ| >> 1, Af ≈ [[A|A β]] = [[1|β]]. The closed-loop gain is completely independent of transistor parameters!
Topic 3

The four negative feedback topologies

Winter 2025 SDC-II Q2Mixing & Sampling Methods
Intuition

Every feedback connection consists of two operations: how output is sampled (voltage vs current) and how feedback is mixed at the input (series vs shunt). 2 × 2 = 4 distinct topologies.

TopologyAlternative NamesOutput SamplingInput MixingFeedback Factor βStabilized Gain
Voltage-SeriesSeries-ShuntVoltage (parallel across output)Series (subtraction of voltages: Vi = Vs − Vf)β = Vf/Vo (dimensionless)Voltage Gain Avf = Vo/Vs
Current-SeriesSeries-SeriesCurrent (in series with load)Series (subtraction of voltages: Vi = Vs − Vf)β = Vf/Io (Ω, Ohms)Transconductance Gmf = Io/Vs
Current-ShuntShunt-SeriesCurrent (in series with load)Shunt (subtraction of currents: Ii = Is − If)β = If/Io (dimensionless)Current Gain Aif = Io/Is
Voltage-ShuntShunt-ShuntVoltage (parallel across output)Shunt (subtraction of currents: Ii = Is − If)β = If/Vo (S, Siemens)Transresistance Rmf = Vo/Is

  2. Draw neat labeled block diagrams for each of the 4 topologies showing source, amplifier, load, and feedback network (4).

Topic 4

Effect on gain stability and bandwidth

Winter 2025 Q4 OR (10 Marks)DesensitizationGain-Bandwidth Product
Intuition

Why do we accept a drop in gain? Because it gives us gain desensitization. If the transistor's internal gain drifts by 20% due to heat or ageing, negative feedback can reduce that variation to less than 0.2%!

4.1 Derivation of Gain Desensitization (Sensitivity)

Starting from the closed-loop gain equation:

A_{f} = [[A|1 + A β]]

Differentiating Af with respect to the open-loop gain A:

[[dA_{f}|dA]] = [[(1 + A β)(1) − A (β)|(1 + A β)^{2}]] = [[1 + A β − A β|(1 + A β)^{2}]] = [[1|(1 + A β)^{2}]]

Divide both sides by Af = A / (1 + Aβ):

[[dA_{f}|A_{f}]] = [[1|1 + A β]] · [[dA|A]]

Conclusion: The fractional percentage change in closed-loop gain ([[dA_{f}|A_{f}]]) is equal to the fractional change in open-loop gain ([[dA|A]]) divided by the desensitivity factor (1 + Aβ)!

4.2 Effect on Bandwidth and Cutoff Frequencies

Consider an amplifier with lower cutoff frequency f1 and upper cutoff frequency f2. Negative feedback affects both ends of the frequency spectrum:

  • Upper Cutoff Frequency (f2): Feedback extends high-frequency response because negative feedback diminishes at high frequencies, flattening the gain curve:
    f_{2f} = f_{2} (1 + A_{mid} β)
  • Lower Cutoff Frequency (f1): Low-frequency roll-off occurs lower down the spectrum:
    f_{1f} = [[f_{1}|1 + A_{mid} β]]
  • Overall Bandwidth (BW):
    BW_{f} = f_{2f} − f_{1f} ≈ f_{2} (1 + A_{mid} β) = BW · (1 + A_{mid} β)
  • Gain-Bandwidth Product (GBW):
    A_{f(mid)} · BW_{f} = [[A_{mid}|1 + A_{mid} β]] · [BW (1 + A_{mid} β)] = A_{mid} · BW = Constant
    The gain-bandwidth product is strictly invariant under feedback. Gain is traded directly for bandwidth!
10-mark answer skeleton (Winter 2025 Q4 OR)
  1. State the feedback gain equation Af = A / (1 + Aβ) (1).
  2. Perform differential calculus step to prove dAf/Af = (1/(1+Aβ)) (dA/A) (3).
  3. Derive high cutoff extension f2f = f2(1+Aβ) and low cutoff f1f = f1/(1+Aβ) (3).
  4. Show that Gain × Bandwidth remains constant (2).
  5. Provide one short numerical calculation illustrating desensitization (1).
Topic 5

Effect on non-linear distortion and noise

Harmonic Distortion DfSignal-to-Noise Ratio
Intuition

When an amplifier operates with large signals, non-linear curvature in transistor characteristics introduces unwanted harmonics (2nd, 3rd harmonics). Negative feedback senses the distortion generated inside the amplifier and subtracts an anti-phase replica at the input, cancelling out the harmonic content!

5.1 Reduction of Non-Linear Distortion

Let D be the harmonic distortion voltage generated within the open-loop amplifier. With negative feedback applied, the distorted component circulating through the loop is returned as β Df and subtracted at the input.

D_{f} = D − A β D_{f} ⇒ D_{f} (1 + A β) = D
D_{f} = [[D|1 + A β]]

Important Caveat: This distortion reduction holds when the closed-loop output signal amplitude is restored to the open-loop level by boosting input excitation, assuming the preceding pre-amplifier stage generates zero distortion.

5.2 Effect of Negative Feedback on Noise

Let noise voltage Vn be generated in an intermediate stage of the amplifier (e.g. power-supply hum, thermal noise). Negative feedback reduces noise generated inside the loop by the factor (1 + Aβ):

V_{nf} = [[V_{n}|1 + A β]]

However, noise entering at the input terminal (such as sensor thermal noise) is amplified by exactly the same closed-loop gain as the signal. Therefore, negative feedback does NOT improve the Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) for input noise. It only suppresses noise generated in subsequent stages within the loop!

Topic 6

Effect on input and output impedance

Winter 2025 Q4 (10 Marks)Summer 2026 Q4 OR (10 Marks)Impedance Derivations
Intuition

Remember the simple rule:

  • Series mixing at input: Opposing feedback voltage Vf opposes Vs, so input current Ii drops dramatically $⇒$ Input impedance increases: Zif = Zi (1 + Aβ).
  • Shunt mixing at input: Feedback current If draws away current in parallel, so from the source's view it takes more total current $⇒$ Input impedance decreases: Zif = Zi / (1 + Aβ).
  • Voltage sampling at output: If output voltage Vo tries to rise, feedback sends a large opposing signal to pull it down (like a stiff voltage regulator) $⇒$ Output impedance decreases: Zof = Zo / (1 + Aβ).
  • Current sampling at output: If output current Io tries to change, feedback opposes the change (like an ideal constant current source) $⇒$ Output impedance increases: Zof = Zo (1 + Aβ).

6.1 Voltage-Series Derivation (Winter 2025 Q4)

Input Impedance (Zif):

V_{s} = V_{i} + V_{f} = I_{i} Z_{i} + β V_{o} = I_{i} Z_{i} + β (A V_{i}) = I_{i} Z_{i} + A β (I_{i} Z_{i}) = I_{i} Z_{i} (1 + A β)
Z_{if} = [[V_{s}|I_{i}]] = Z_{i} (1 + A β)

Output Impedance (Zof): To find Zof, set signal source Vs = 0 and apply an external test voltage V at the output port:

V_{s} = 0 ⇒ V_{i} = −V_{f} = −β V

The current I delivered by test source into the output port:

I = [[V − A V_{i}|Z_{o}]] = [[V − A(−β V)|Z_{o}]] = [[V (1 + A β)|Z_{o}]]
Z_{of} = [[V|I]] = [[Z_{o}|1 + A β]]

6.2 Current-Shunt Derivation (Summer 2026 Q4 OR)

Input Impedance (Zif): Shunt mixing connects feedback current in parallel with input:

I_{s} = I_{i} + I_{f} = I_{i} + β I_{o} = I_{i} + β (A_{i} I_{i}) = I_{i} (1 + A_{i} β)

Since input voltage is Vi = Ii Zi:

Z_{if} = [[V_{i}|I_{s}]] = [[I_{i} Z_{i}|I_{i} (1 + A_{i} β)]] = [[Z_{i}|1 + A_{i} β]]

Output Impedance (Zof): Current sampling increases output impedance:

Z_{of} = Z_{o} (1 + A_{i} β)
Feedback TopologyInput ConnectionOutput ConnectionInput Impedance ZifOutput Impedance Zof
Voltage-SeriesSeriesShunt (voltage)Zi (1 + Avβ) [Increases]Zo / (1 + Avβ) [Decreases]
Voltage-ShuntShuntShunt (voltage)Zi / (1 + Rmβ) [Decreases]Zo / (1 + Rmβ) [Decreases]
Current-SeriesSeriesSeries (current)Zi (1 + Gmβ) [Increases]Zo (1 + Gmβ) [Increases]
Current-ShuntShuntSeries (current)Zi / (1 + Aiβ) [Decreases]Zo (1 + Aiβ) [Increases]
10-mark answer skeleton (Winter 2025 Q4 / Summer 2026 Q4 OR)
  1. State the general definition of input/output impedance with feedback (1).
  2. Draw AC equivalent circuit with test generator V at output (Vs = 0) (3).
  3. Derive step-by-step Zof = Zo / (1+Aβ) showing Vi = −βV (3).
  4. Derive Zif = Zi(1+Aβ) for series mixing or Zif = Zi/(1+Aβ) for shunt mixing (3).
Topic 7

BJT voltage-series feedback amplifier

Two-Stage RC-Coupled CEExact Notes Worked Example
Intuition

To implement voltage-series feedback with BJTs, why do we use a two-stage amplifier? Because each CE stage introduces a 180° phase inversion. Two cascaded CE stages produce 180° + 180° = 360° (0°) overall phase shift. When the output voltage at the second collector is fed back to the emitter of the first stage, the emitter voltage rises in phase with the base signal, which subtracts from Vbe1 (Vbe1 = Vb1 − Ve1), creating true negative feedback!

7.1 Circuit Analysis & Formulas

The feedback factor β is set by the resistive divider from Q2 collector to Q1 emitter:

β = [[R_{f2}|R_{f1} + R_{f2}]]

The closed-loop voltage gain for large loop gain:

A_{vf} = [[A_{v}|1 + A_{v} β]] ≈ [[1|β]] = [[R_{f1} + R_{f2}|R_{f2}]] = 1 + [[R_{f1}|R_{f2}]]

7.2 Solved Problem: Exact Numerical Example from Dr. Ghongade's Notes

Worked Example 1 (From Handwritten Teaching Notes)

Given Data: A two-stage RC-coupled CE BJT amplifier has open-loop voltage gain Av = 1118, input resistance Zi = 1.98 kΩ, and output resistance Zo = 12 kΩ. The feedback network uses Rf1 = 16.2 kΩ and Rf2 = 220 Ω.

  1. Calculate feedback factor β:
    β = [[R_{f2}|R_{f1} + R_{f2}]] = [[220|16200 + 220]] = [[220|16420]] = 0.0134
  2. Calculate loop gain and desensitivity factor:
    A_{v} β = 1118 × 0.0134 = 14.98 ⇒ 1 + A_{v} β = 1 + 14.98 = 15.98
  3. Calculate closed-loop voltage gain Avf:
    A_{vf} = [[A_{v}|1 + A_{v} β]] = [[1118|15.98]] = 69.96 ≈ 70
    Check with approximation: 1/β = 16420 / 220 = 74.6.
  4. Calculate closed-loop input impedance:
    Z_{if} = Z_{i} (1 + A_{v} β) = 1.98 kΩ × 15.98 = 31.64 kΩ
  5. Calculate closed-loop output impedance:
    Z_{of} = [[Z_{o}|1 + A_{v} β]] = [[12 kΩ|15.98]] = 750.9 Ω

7.3 Complete Design Procedure (Capacitor Selection at fL = 100 Hz)

From the notes, for fL = 100 Hz:

  • Bypass capacitor C2 at Rf2:
    |X_{C2}| = R_{f2} ⇒ C_{2} = [[1|2 π f_{L} R_{f2}]] = [[1|2 π (100) (220)]] = 7.23 µF ⇒ Select Standard 8 µF or 10 µF
  • Decoupling capacitor Cf1:
    C_{f1} = [[1|2 π f_{L} (R_{f1}/100)]] = [[1|2 π (100) (162)]] = 9.8 µF ⇒ Select Standard 10 µF
Topic 8

BJT current-series feedback amplifier

CE with Unbypassed RE1Gain Stabilization6-Step Design
Intuition

The simplest and most widely used negative feedback circuit in electronics is a single CE transistor stage where the emitter resistor is split into two parts: an unbypassed resistor RE1 and a bypassed resistor RE2. The emitter current IE flows through RE1, developing a feedback voltage Vf = IE RE1 directly in series with the input!

8.1 Analytical Derivations

1. Transistor Base Input Impedance (Zb):

Z_{b} = h_{ie} + (1 + h_{fe}) R_{E1}

Total amplifier input impedance:

Z_{in} = R_{1} || R_{2} || Z_{b}

2. Small-Signal Voltage Gain:

A_{v} = − [[h_{fe} (R_{C} || R_{L})|h_{ie} + (1 + h_{fe}) R_{E1}]]

When (1 + hfe)RE1 >> hie and since hfe/(1+hfe) ≈ 1:

A_{v} ≈ − [[R_{C} || R_{L}|R_{E1}]]

Notice that the voltage gain depends only on passive circuit resistors and is virtually independent of hfe!

8.2 Solved Example: Exact hfe Variation from Dr. Ghongade's Notes

Worked Example 2 (From Notes)

Circuit values: RC = 12 kΩ, RL = 120 kΩ (giving RC∥RL = 10.91 kΩ), RE1 = 150 Ω.
Test the gain stability across extreme transistor production spreads:

  • At maximum parameters: hfe = 400, hie = 5 kΩ:
    |A_{v(max)}| = [[400 (10.91 kΩ)|5 kΩ + (1 + 400)(150 Ω)]] = [[4364|5 + 60.15]] = 67.0
  • At minimum parameters: hfe = 100, hie = 2 kΩ:
    |A_{v(min)}| = [[100 (10.91 kΩ)|2 kΩ + (1 + 100)(150 Ω)]] = [[1091|2 + 15.15]] = 63.6

Result: While hfe varied by 300% (from 100 to 400), the voltage gain changed by only 5.3% (from 63.6 to 67.0)! This proves the immense gain-stabilizing power of emitter-current feedback.

8.3 Exact 6-Step Design Example (from Notes)

Requirement: Design a current-series amplifier for Av = 70, fL = 100 Hz, with Rs = 600 Ω, hie = 2 kΩ, hfe = 100, VCC = 24 V, R1 = 120 kΩ, R2 = 39 kΩ, RC = 12 kΩ, RL = 120 kΩ, original RE = 3.9 kΩ.

  1. Calculate RE1:
    R_{E1} ≈ [[R_{C} || R_{L}|A_{v}]] = [[10.91 kΩ|70]] = 155.8 Ω ⇒ Select Standard 150 Ω
  2. Calculate bypassed RE2:
    R_{E2} = R_{E(orig)} − R_{E1} = 3.9 kΩ − 150 Ω = 3.75 kΩ ⇒ Select Standard 3.9 kΩ
  3. Calculate input impedance at base Zb:
    Z_{b} = h_{ie} + (1 + h_{fe}) R_{E1} = 2 kΩ + (101)(150 Ω) = 17.15 kΩ
  4. Calculate total input impedance Zin:
    Z_{in} = 120 kΩ || 39 kΩ || 17.15 kΩ ≈ 10.8 kΩ
  5. Calculate input coupling capacitor C1:
    |X_{C1}| = [[Z_{in} + R_{s}|10]] ⇒ C_{1} = [[1|2 π (100) (1140)]] = 1.39 µF ⇒ Select Standard 1.5 µF
  6. Calculate emitter bypass capacitor C2:
    |X_{C2}| = R_{E1} ⇒ C_{2} = [[1|2 π (100) (150)]] = 10.61 µF ⇒ Select Standard 12 µF
Topic 9

BJT current-shunt feedback amplifier

DC Feedback PairCurrent Gain StabilizationDesign Example
Intuition

The current-shunt feedback configuration uses a two-transistor DC feedback pair. The output current Io in the emitter of Q2 is sampled through resistor Rf2, and feedback current If is returned in parallel (shunt) to the base of Q1 through Rf1. This configuration lowers input impedance to tens of ohms, making it an outstanding current amplifier!

9.1 Circuit Operation & Derivations

At the input node, source current splits: is = ii + if. At the feedback sampling point, output current splits between Rf1 and Rf2:

β = [[R_{f2}|R_{f1} + R_{f2}]]

Closed-loop current gain:

A_{if} = [[I_{o}|I_{s}]] = [[A_{i}|1 + A_{i} β]] ≈ [[1|β]] = [[R_{f1} + R_{f2}|R_{f2}]]

Input impedance with feedback:

Z_{if} = [[h_{ie1}|1 + A_{i} β]]

9.2 Solved Design Example from Dr. Ghongade's Notes

Worked Example 3 (From Notes)

Specification: Design a current-shunt feedback amplifier to achieve closed-loop current gain Aif = 55 using the designed DC pair (VCC = 12 V, IC = 2 mA, hfe = 100, hie = 2 kΩ, Ai(open) = 6226).

  1. Obtain feedback resistor ratio:
    A_{if} ≈ [[R_{f1} + R_{f2}|R_{f2}]] = 55 ⇒ [[R_{f1}|R_{f2}]] = 54 ⇒ R_{f1} = 54 R_{f2}
  2. Select standard Rf2 and compute Rf1:

    As per the notes, choose Rf2 between 100 Ω and 470 Ω. Let Rf2 = 100 Ω:

    R_{f1} = 54 × 100 Ω = 5.4 kΩ ⇒ Select Standard 5.6 kΩ
  3. Split original bias resistor R2 = 180 kΩ:
    R_{5} = R_{2} − R_{f1} = 180 kΩ − 5.6 kΩ = 174.4 kΩ ⇒ Select Standard 180 kΩ
  4. Calculate feedback coupling capacitor Cf1 at fL = 150 Hz:
    |X_{Cf1}| = [[R_{f1}|100]] ⇒ Selected in notes: C_{f1} = 2.2 µF
  5. Calculate input impedance under feedback:
    Z_{if} = [[h_{ie1}|1 + A_{i} β]] = [[2000 Ω|1 + [[6226|55]]]] = [[2000|1 + 113.2]] = 17.5 Ω
Topic 10

BJT voltage-shunt feedback amplifier

Collector-to-Base FeedbackTransresistance Stabilization
Intuition

In a voltage-shunt feedback amplifier, a feedback resistor Rf is connected directly from the collector (output voltage) back to the base (input current) of a CE stage. Output voltage is sampled, and feedback is returned in shunt. Both input and output impedances are drastically reduced, and the circuit acts as a highly stable transresistance amplifier!

10.1 Circuit Analysis & Formulas

Feedback current returning to base:

I_{f} = [[V_{i} − V_{o}|R_{f}]] ≈ − [[V_{o}|R_{f}]] = β V_{o} ⇒ β = − [[1|R_{f}]]

The open-loop transresistance Rm without feedback is defined as Vo / Is:

R = R_{s} || R_{f1}, R_{C}' = R_{C} || R_{f2}
R_{m} = − [[h_{fe} R_{C}' R|R + h_{ie}]]

The closed-loop transresistance:

R_{mf} = [[R_{m}|1 + β R_{m}]] = − [[h_{fe} R_{C}' R R_{f}|R_{f}(R + h_{ie}) + h_{fe} R_{C}' R]]

When loop gain is very large, Rmf simplifies to:

R_{mf} ≈ [[1|β]] = − R_{f}

The overall voltage gain is simply:

A_{vf} = [[V_{o}|V_{s}]] = [[R_{mf}|R_{s}]] ≈ − [[R_{f}|R_{s}]]
Topic 11

Consolidated summary & topology identification rule

3-Step Identification AlgorithmMaster Comparison
Intuition

Exam questions often ask you to classify an unfamiliar circuit. Follow this infallible 3-step decision procedure:

  1. Determine Output Sampling: Look at the output node.
    • If feedback network is connected directly in parallel with the output terminals (so setting Vo = 0 removes the feedback signal), it is Voltage Sampling.
    • If feedback network is in series with the load (so opening the load loop reduces feedback signal to zero), it is Current Sampling.
  2. Determine Input Mixing: Look at the input node.
    • If feedback signal is in series with the external source (loops around base-emitter loop: Vi = Vs − Vf), it is Series Mixing.
    • If feedback signal connects directly to the same node as the input source (KCL: Ii = Is − If), it is Shunt Mixing.
  3. Apply the Impedance Rule:
    • Series mixing $⇒$ Zin multiplies by (1 + Aβ).
    • Shunt mixing $⇒$ Zin divides by (1 + Aβ).
    • Voltage sampling $⇒$ Zout divides by (1 + Aβ).
    • Current sampling $⇒$ Zout multiplies by (1 + Aβ).
TopologyTypical CircuitZinZoutStabilized QuantityPractical Application
Voltage-SeriesTwo-stage CE with Rf1-Rf2 from Q2 collector to Q1 emitterHigh: Zi(1+Aβ)Low: Zo/(1+Aβ)Voltage Gain AvfAudio preamplifiers, op-amp non-inverting buffers
Current-SeriesSingle CE with unbypassed emitter resistor RE1High: Zi(1+Aβ)High: ≈ RCTransconductance GmfTransconductance stages, voltage-to-current converters
Current-ShuntTwo-transistor DC feedback pairLow: Zi/(1+Aβ)High: Zo(1+Aβ)Current Gain AifCurrent amplifiers, transducer current summing
Voltage-ShuntCollector-to-base feedback resistor RfLow: Zi/(1+Aβ)Low: Zo/(1+Aβ)Transresistance RmfTransimpedance amplifiers (TIA), photodiode amplifiers

S0 · Orientation

Start Here: Core Intuitions of Power Amplifiers

Core FoundationsLarge-Signal PhysicsEfficiency vs Distortion
Intuition 1 · Small Signal vs Large Signal: Why Models Break Down

In small-signal amplifiers (Units 1 to 4), input signals are tiny (a few millivolts). The transistor operates over a microscopic slice of its dynamic characteristics where curves can be treated as perfectly straight lines. Linear models like h-parameters or re work beautifully. But in power amplifiers (large-signal amplifiers), the base voltage swings across the entire active region—from near cut-off right up to saturation. Transistor non-linearities dominate, collector current swings reach several Amperes, and internal power dissipation creates intense heat. Efficiency [[P_{ac}|P_{dc}]] and thermal dissipation become the governing design parameters rather than voltage gain.

Intuition 2 · The Universal Efficiency vs Distortion Trade-off

Every power amplifier is governed by an inescapable physics compromise between conduction angle and efficiency:

  • Class A (θc = 360°): Transistor stays ON during the entire AC cycle. Zero crossover distortion, purest audio reproduction. But because it draws heavy DC quiescent current even when completely idle, theoretical efficiency is capped at 25% (series-fed) or 50% (transformer-coupled). Up to 75% of power is wasted as pure heat.
  • Class B (θc = 180°): Biased exactly at cut-off. Zero quiescent idle current! Theoretical efficiency leaps to 78.5%. But because silicon transistors need 0.7V of base-emitter voltage to turn on, a dead zone appears at every zero crossing—causing harsh crossover distortion.
  • Class AB (180° < θc < 360°): Biased slightly above cut-off (idle current ~5% to 10% of peak). Eliminates the 0.7V dead band while delivering 50% to 70% efficiency. This is the bedrock of modern commercial audio power stages.
  • Class C (θc < 180°): Conducts only in brief pulses (~90°). Extremely high efficiency (~85%), but severe distortion makes it unusable for direct audio. Essential for RF transmitters where resonant LC tank circuits restore sinusoidal purity via flywheel effect.
  • Class D (Switching / PWM): Transistors operate strictly as ON/OFF switches (saturation or cut-off). Because either voltage across the switch is zero or current through it is zero, theoretical internal dissipation is zero! Achieves 90% to 98% efficiency.
Intuition 3 · The Miracle of Push-Pull Symmetry

In single-ended amplifiers, non-linear transfer curves introduce substantial second-harmonic (even-order) distortion. In a push-pull pair, two matched transistors are driven with anti-phase signals (180° apart) into a center-tapped transformer or complementary load. When their collector currents are subtracted in the load winding:
inet = iC1 − iC2 = 2·I1·sin(ωt) + 2·I3·sin(3ωt) + ...
All even harmonics (2nd, 4th, 6th) and DC core-magnetizing currents cancel out identically! Furthermore, power supply ripple is rejected, and core saturation is completely avoided.

Intuition 4 · Impedance Matching & Damping Factor

Loudspeakers have low voice-coil impedances: typically 4 Ω, 8 Ω, or 16 Ω. A standard common-emitter amplifier has an output impedance of several kilo-ohms. Connecting an 8 Ω speaker directly to a 2 kΩ collector resistor would cause 99.6% of signal power to be lost inside the amplifier! Power amplifiers solve this either through an impedance matching transformer with turns ratio [[N_{1}|N_{2}]] = √[[R'_{L}|R_{L}]], or via a complementary emitter follower whose output impedance is naturally tiny (< 0.5 Ω), providing massive damping factor to prevent speaker cone ringing.

Topic 1

Need of Power Amplifiers & System Signal Chain

Audio Engineering ArchitectureVoltage vs Power AmplifiersImpedance Matching
System Perspective

Why can't a microphone drive a loudspeaker directly? Why do audio systems require multiple cascaded stages? Let's trace the signal journey from acoustic wave to amplified sound.

1.1 Walkthrough of the Signal Chain

  1. Input Transducer (Microphone): Converts acoustic pressure waves into electrical signals. Output voltage is minute—typically few microvolts (µV) to millivolts (mV) (e.g. 1 mV to 10 mV). The source impedance is high (typically 600 Ω to 50 kΩ), and available power is on the order of picowatts (pW).
  2. Small-Signal Voltage Amplifier (Preamplifier): Designed for maximum voltage gain (Av ≈ 100 to 1000) with minimal noise figure. It boosts the delicate mV input up to 1 V to 2 V. However, its collector resistor is relatively large (several kΩ), meaning it cannot supply more than a few milliamperes of current without clipping or collapsing.
  3. Driver / Phase Splitter Stage: Serves as an impedance buffer between the high-gain preamp and the heavy-current power transistors. In push-pull designs, it produces two equal-amplitude, perfectly 180° anti-phase drive signals (v1 and v2 = −v1).
  4. Large-Signal Power Amplifier (Output Stage): Takes the 1V–2V signal and provides massive current gain (Ai >> 1) while preserving voltage swing. Its output impedance is extremely low (< 1 Ω), enabling it to deliver Amperes of peak current and tens to hundreds of Watts into the low speaker impedance without voltage sagging.
  5. Output Transducer (Loudspeaker): Electromagnetic voice coil suspended in a permanent magnet field. Typical voice-coil resistances are 4 Ω, 8 Ω, 16 Ω, or 32 Ω. Converts high-current electrical power into mechanical cone vibrations, projecting acoustic power into the room.

1.2 Fundamental Differences: Voltage vs Power Amplifiers

Design ParameterSmall-Signal Voltage AmplifierLarge-Signal Power Amplifier
Primary ObjectiveAchieve maximum voltage gain Av = Vo/ViDeliver maximum AC power Pac = Vrms·Irms to load
Input Signal LevelTiny (microvolts to millivolts)Substantial (1 V to several Volts peak)
Collector Current (IC)Small (typically 0.5 mA to 5 mA)Large (hundreds of mA to tens of Amperes)
Operating RegionMicroscopic linear region around Q-pointWide excursion across full load line (cut-off to saturation)
Mathematical ModelingLinear two-port models (h-parameters, re)Graphical load line analysis & harmonic series
Transistor ConstructionSmall surface area, thin base, low power rating (< 500 mW)Broad base-collector junction area, metal TO-3/TO-220 cases (> 10W to 150W)
Output Impedance (Zout)Relatively high (comparable to RC, 1 kΩ to 10 kΩ)Extremely low (< 0.5 Ω) to drive 4Ω–16Ω loads
Collector Efficiency (η)Unimportant (idle power is negligible, few mW)Critical (determines battery life, heat dissipation, heatsink size)
Thermal Design / HeatsinkNo heatsink required (air cooling sufficient)Extensive aluminum finned heatsinks with thermal paste mandatory
10-Mark Answer Skeleton · Voltage vs Power Amplifiers
  1. Introduction & Block Diagram (3 marks): Draw the complete audio system block diagram (Mic → Voltage Amp → Driver → Power Amp → Speaker). State the signal levels (µV/mV → 1–2V → Watts).
  2. Functional Definition (2 marks): Define power amplifier as a large-signal converter that transforms DC power supplied by the bias source into AC signal power delivered to a low-impedance load with high collector circuit efficiency.
  3. Detailed Comparison Table (3 marks): Reproduce at least 6 key distinguishing parameters from the comparison table (Objective, IC range, Zout, efficiency, heatsink, modeling).
  4. Impedance Mismatch Problem (2 marks): Explain mathematically why connecting an 8 Ω speaker directly to a 2 kΩ collector stage causes 99% signal loss, necessitating impedance transformation.
Quick check: Why are h-parameters invalid for designing large-signal power amplifiers?

h-parameters (hie, hfe, hoe, hre) are strictly small-signal differential derivatives evaluated at a static operating point (e.g. hfe = ∂iC/∂iB). In a power amplifier, the operating point swings wildly from cut-off to saturation, where hfe drops drastically, base-emitter resistance changes non-linearly, and junction temperatures rise. Therefore, graphical load line analysis and large-signal parameters must be used.

Topic 2

Amplifier Classification: Conduction Angle & Operating Region

Winter 2025 SDC-II Q3 (10 Marks)Conduction Angle θcEfficiency Spectrum
Intuition

The classification of an amplifier is defined solely by its conduction angle θc—the portion of the 360° input sine-wave cycle during which collector current flows through the active transistor. By shifting the DC Q-point along the dynamic transfer curve from the center of the active region down past cut-off, we trade off harmonic fidelity for electrical efficiency.

2.1 Comprehensive Analysis of Amplifier Classes

  1. Class A Amplifier (θc = 360° or 2π radians):
    • Bias Point: Biased in the center of the linear active region: VCEQ ≈ [[V_{CC}|2]] (series-fed) or VCEQ = VCC (transformer-coupled).
    • Collector Current: Flows continuously for the entire 360° cycle of the input signal (never drops to zero).
    • Output Waveform: Faithful reproduction of input signal with minimal harmonic distortion.
    • Collector Efficiency: Very poor. Maximum theoretical efficiency is 25% for series-fed resistive load and 50% for transformer-coupled load.
    • Thermal Behavior: Heavy idle current dissipation (PQ = VCEQ·ICQ). The transistor runs hottest when NO audio signal is playing!
    • Application: High-fidelity audiophile preamplifiers, low-power RF driver stages, instrumentation amplifiers.
  2. Class B Amplifier (θc = 180° or π radians):
    • Bias Point: Biased precisely at cut-off: ICQ = 0, VBEQ ≈ 0 V.
    • Collector Current: Flows for exactly one half-cycle (180°) of the AC input signal. Transistor is completely turned OFF during the negative half.
    • Output Waveform: A single transistor delivers only half-wave pulses. A push-pull pair (two transistors operating 180° out of phase) is required to reconstruct the complete sine wave.
    • Collector Efficiency: High. Maximum theoretical efficiency is [[π|4]] ≈ 78.54%.
    • Distortion: Suffers from crossover distortion near zero crossings due to the 0.7 V cut-in threshold of silicon BJTs.
    • Thermal Behavior: Zero quiescent power dissipation when signal is absent. Transistor dissipation is proportional to output level.
    • Application: Battery-operated intercoms, public address systems where battery conservation is critical.
  3. Class AB Amplifier (180° < θc < 360°):
    • Bias Point: Biased slightly above cut-off (small quiescent current ICQ ≈ 5% to 10% of peak collector current Ip).
    • Collector Current: Flows for more than half a cycle but less than the full cycle (typically 200° to 220°).
    • Output Waveform: Both transistors conduct simultaneously near the zero crossing, creating a seamless hand-off that completely eliminates crossover distortion.
    • Collector Efficiency: Typically 50% to 70% (theoretical upper limit 78.5%).
    • Advantages: Combines the linearity and purity of Class A with the high efficiency and low idle power of Class B.
    • Application: The universal industry standard for consumer Hi-Fi audio amplifiers, studio monitors, guitar power amps.
  4. Class C Amplifier (θc < 180°, typically 80° to 120°):
    • Bias Point: Biased well below cut-off using reverse base-emitter bias (VBEQ < 0 V).
    • Collector Current: Flows in narrow pulses during the positive peak of the input cycle.
    • Collector Efficiency: Highest among linear-biased circuits, typically 80% to 90%.
    • Distortion: Severe non-linear distortion. Completely unusable for direct audio amplification.
    • Application: Radio Frequency (RF) power transmitters (AM, FM, mobile transmitters). A parallel LC resonant tank circuit forms the collector load, which rings sinusoidally via the "flywheel effect" to reconstruct a pure carrier wave.
  5. Class D Amplifier (Switching / Pulse-Width Modulation):
    • Operating Principle: Non-linear switching amplifier. The audio input is compared against a high-frequency triangular wave (e.g. 250 kHz to 1 MHz) to generate a Pulse-Width Modulated (PWM) square wave.
    • Transistor State: Output MOSFETs operate strictly as rapid electronic switches: either fully ON (saturation, where VDS ≈ 0 V) or fully OFF (cut-off, where ID = 0 A).
    • Efficiency: Since Ploss = VDS · ID is approximately zero in both states, theoretical efficiency approaches 100% (practical commercial circuits achieve 90% to 98%).
    • Reconstruction: A passive low-pass LC Butterworth filter removes the high-frequency switching carrier, delivering the pure amplified audio signal to the loudspeaker.
    • Application: Modern smartphones, automotive audio systems, active subwoofers, ultra-compact portable Bluetooth speakers.

2.2 Master Comparison Table of Amplifier Classes

Classification CriteriaClass AClass BClass ABClass CClass D
Operating Point (Q-Point)Center of active regionExactly at cut-offSlightly above cut-offWell below cut-offSwitching (Cut-off / Sat.)
Conduction Angle (θc)360° (2π)180° (π)180° < θc < 360° (~210°)< 180° (typically 90°)Switching pulses (PWM)
Theoretical Max Efficiency (η)25% (series), 50% (xfmr)78.54% ([[π|4]])50% to 78.5%85% to 90%95% to 100%
Practical Efficiency15% to 35%60% to 70%50% to 65%75% to 85%88% to 95%

10-Mark Exam Skeleton · Winter 2025 SDC-II Q3
  1. Define Conduction Angle (2 marks): State the formal definition of θc and how the Q-point location determines the conduction interval for Class A, B, and C.
  2. Transfer Characteristics & Waveforms (4 marks): Draw the dynamic transfer curves (iC vs iB or vin) showing Q-point placement and resulting collector current output waveforms for Class A (360°), Class B (180°), and Class C (<180°).
  3. Analytical Comparison (4 marks): Provide a clear table comparing Class A, B, and C across at least 5 parameters: Q-point, conduction angle, theoretical efficiency derivation limits (25/50% vs 78.5% vs >85%), distortion levels, and typical applications.
Quick check: Why can't a Class C amplifier be used directly as an audio power amplifier?

In Class C, current flows for only a fraction of the cycle (θc < 180°), clipping more than half of the input audio waveform completely. In RF amplifiers, an LC resonant circuit tuned to the carrier frequency reconstructs the missing portions via electromagnetic oscillation (flywheel effect). In audio, the signal spans three decades of frequencies (20 Hz to 20 kHz), making tuned LC reconstruction impossible; the output would sound intolerably distorted.

Topic 3

Series-Fed Class A Power Amplifier

Winter 2025 Q5 OR (10 Marks)DC Load LineEfficiency Derivation (25%)Figure of Merit = 2
Circuit Concept

The series-fed Class A amplifier is the simplest large-signal topology: the load resistor RL is connected directly in series with the collector of an NPN power BJT. Because current flows continuously through both RL and the transistor, it provides our foundational benchmark for power, dissipation, and efficiency limits.

3.1 Circuit Equations & Load Line Analysis

Applying Kirchhoff's Voltage Law (KVL) to the collector-emitter output loop:

V_{CC} − I_{C} R_{L} − V_{CE} = 0 ⇒ I_{C} = [[V_{CC} − V_{CE}|R_{L}]] = − [[1|R_{L}]] V_{CE} + [[V_{CC}|R_{L}]]

This is the equation of a straight line (the DC Load Line) plotted on the IC versus VCE output characteristics:

  • Cut-off Point (Point A): When transistor is cut off, IC = 0 $⇒$ VCE(cut-off) = VCC. Coordinates: (VCC, 0).
  • Saturation Point (Point B): When transistor saturates, VCE = 0 $⇒$ IC(sat) = [[V_{CC}|R_{L}]]. Coordinates: (0, [[V_{CC}|R_{L}]]).
  • Optimal Q-Point for Maximum Symmetrical Swing: To prevent premature clipping on either peak, the quiescent operating point Q must be located at the precise midpoint of the load line:
V_{CEQ} = [[V_{CC}|2]], I_{CQ} = [[V_{CC}|2 R_{L}]]

3.2 Derivation of Maximum AC Output Power (Pac(max))

When an AC input signal is applied, the operating point swings symmetrically up and down along the load line. Under maximum undistorted excursion:

  • Peak-to-peak output voltage swing: Vopp = VCE(max) − VCE(min) = VCC − 0 = VCC.
  • Peak output voltage: Vp = [[V_{opp}|2]] = [[V_{CC}|2]].
  • RMS output voltage: Vrms = [[V_{p}|√2]] = [[V_{CC}|2√2]].
  • Peak-to-peak output current swing: Iopp = IC(max) − IC(min) = [[V_{CC}|R_{L}]] − 0 = [[V_{CC}|R_{L}]].
  • Peak output current: Ip = [[I_{opp}|2]] = [[V_{CC}|2 R_{L}]].
  • RMS output current: Irms = [[I_{p}|√2]] = [[V_{CC}|2√2 R_{L}]].

The AC power delivered to the load resistor RL is:

P_{ac} = V_{rms} · I_{rms} = [[V_{opp}|2√2]] · [[I_{opp}|2√2]] = [[(V_{max} − V_{min})(I_{max} − I_{min})|8]]

Expressed purely in terms of peak-to-peak voltage across load resistance RL:

P_{ac(max)} = [[V_{opp}^2|8 R_{L}]] = [[(V_{CC})^2|8 R_{L}]] = [[V_{CC}^2|8 R_{L}]]

3.3 Derivation of DC Input Power (Pdc)

The DC power supplied by the battery source VCC is the product of supply voltage and average DC current drawn from it. Because the output current swings sinusoidally around the quiescent bias level ICQ, the average (DC) collector current remains constant at ICQ:

P_{dc} = V_{CC} · I_{CQ} = V_{CC} · [[V_{CC}|2 R_{L}]] = [[V_{CC}^2|2 R_{L}]]

3.4 Derivation of Maximum Collector Efficiency (ηmax)

Collector circuit efficiency η is defined as the ratio of AC output power delivered to the load to the total DC power supplied by the source:

η_{max} = [[P_{ac(max)}|P_{dc}]] × 100% = [[[[V_{CC}^2|8 R_{L}]]|[[V_{CC}^2|2 R_{L}]]]] × 100% = [[2|8]] × 100% = 25%

Physical Conclusion: In a series-fed Class A amplifier, even under optimal, zero-saturation conditions, at most 25% of the DC power supplied is converted into audio output power. The remaining 75% is entirely dissipated as waste heat in the transistor and load resistor.

3.5 Power Dissipation & Figure of Merit (FM)

The power dissipated within the transistor itself is the difference between total DC input power and AC power delivered to the external load:

P_{D} = P_{dc} − P_{ac}
  • Quiescent Condition (Zero Input Signal, Pac = 0):
    P_{Q(max)} = V_{CEQ} · I_{CQ} = [[V_{CC}|2]] · [[V_{CC}|2 R_{L}]] = [[V_{CC}^2|4 R_{L}]]
    The transistor dissipates its maximum possible power when idling! As the audio signal level increases, AC power is delivered to the load, and the internal dissipation of the transistor actually decreases.
  • Figure of Merit (FM): Defined as the ratio of maximum transistor power dissipation to maximum AC output power:
    FM = [[P_{Q(max)}|P_{ac(max)}]] = [[[[V_{CC}^2|4 R_{L}]]|[[V_{CC}^2|8 R_{L}]]]] = 2
    Physical Significance: The transistor must be rated to safely dissipate at least twice the maximum AC power it can deliver to the load (e.g. to produce 10 W of audio power, you must select a transistor rated for at least 20 W of continuous thermal dissipation).

3.6 Practical Efficiency with Saturation Voltage Vce(min)

In a real power transistor, collector voltage cannot drop to zero; it is bounded by the collector saturation voltage VCE(sat) = V_{ce(min)} (typically 0.5 V to 2 V). The practical voltage swing becomes Vopp = VCC − Vce(min), reducing efficiency to:

η = 25% · ([[V_{CC} − V_{ce(min)}|V_{CC}]])^2 or η = [[(V_{max} − V_{min})^2|8 · V_{CC} · V_{CEQ}]] × 100%
Interactive Simulation · Class A Efficiency Analyzer
