10-Mark Answer Skeleton · Series-Fed Class A Amplifier
  1. Circuit Diagram & Operation (2 marks): Draw the circuit schematic of series-fed Class A amplifier. Explain role of RB, RL, C1, and VCC.
  2. DC Load Line Analysis (2 marks): Derive cut-off point (VCC, 0) and saturation point (0, VCC/RL). State condition for Q-point centering at (VCC/2, VCC/2RL).
  3. Power Derivations (4 marks): Formulate Vrms, Irms, derive Pac(max) = VCC2 / (8RL), derive Pdc = VCC2 / (2RL), and prove ηmax = 25%.
  4. Power Dissipation & FM (2 marks): Prove PQ(max) = VCC2 / (4RL) at zero signal. Calculate Figure of Merit FM = PQ / Pac = 2.
Quick check: Why does a Class A amplifier run cooler when playing loud audio than when silent?

The total DC power Pdc drawn from the supply is constant at VCC · ICQ regardless of signal level. Transistor dissipation is PD = Pdc − Pac. When silent (Pac = 0), all DC power is converted to heat inside the transistor (PQ = VCC2 / 4RL). When audio plays at full volume, 25% of that power is transferred to the external speaker as sound and voice-coil heat, leaving only 75% inside the amplifier.

Topic 4

Transformer-Coupled Class A Power Amplifier

Winter 2025 Q5 OR (10 Marks)Impedance Matching2VCC Voltage Swing50% Max Efficiency
Why Use a Transformer?

The series-fed Class A amplifier suffered two crippling weaknesses: (1) low efficiency (25%) because DC current burned power continuously in RL, and (2) inability to drive low-impedance loads (4Ω–16Ω) directly. The transformer-coupled Class A amplifier solves both: the primary winding has near-zero DC resistance (eliminating quiescent DC resistive loss), while its step-down turns ratio transforms the tiny speaker impedance into an optimal large reflected load R'L. Most remarkably, the collector voltage swings all the way up to 2·VCC due to inductive energy storage!

4.1 Reflected Load Impedance (R'L)

Let the output transformer have N1 primary turns and N2 secondary turns, giving turns ratio n = [[N_{1}|N_{2}]]. Across ideal transformer terminals:

[[V_{1}|V_{2}]] = [[N_{1}|N_{2}]] = n ⇒ V_{1} = n V_{2}, [[I_{1}|I_{2}]] = [[N_{2}|N_{1}]] = [[1|n]] ⇒ I_{1} = [[I_{2}|n]]

The effective AC load impedance seen looking into the primary winding is:

R'_{L} = [[V_{1}|I_{1}]] = [[n V_{2}|I_{2} / n]] = n^2 · [[V_{2}|I_{2}]] = ([[N_{1}|N_{2}]])^2 · R_{L}

If primary winding wire has non-zero DC resistance Rpy, the total AC load is R''L = R'L + Rpy.

4.2 DC & AC Load Line Dynamics: Why Voltage Reaches 2·VCC

  1. Vertical DC Load Line: The DC winding resistance of the transformer primary is negligible (Rdc ≈ 0 Ω). Applying KVL under DC conditions:
    V_{CEQ} = V_{CC} − I_{CQ} · R_{dc} ≈ V_{CC}
    The DC load line is a perfectly vertical line at VCE = VCC! Unlike the series-fed stage where VCEQ = VCC/2, the transistor now enjoys the full supply voltage VCC at rest.
  2. AC Load Line: For AC signals, the primary presents impedance R'L. The AC load line passes through the quiescent point Q(VCC, I_{CQ}) with slope −[[1|R'_{L}]].
  3. The Inductive 2·VCC Mechanism: When the input AC signal drives base current downwards toward cut-off (IC drops from ICQ to 0), the magnetic field in the transformer primary core collapses. According to Lenz's Law, the inductor induces a voltage vL = L·[[di|dt]] that opposes the decay of current, effectively adding in series with the battery VCC:
    V_{CE(max)} = V_{CC} + I_{CQ} · R'_{L} = V_{CC} + V_{CC} = 2 · V_{CC}
    The collector-emitter voltage swings symmetrically from 0 V up to 2·VCC!
  4. Symmetrical Current Excursion: When VCE drops to zero during the opposite peak, collector current reaches:
    I_{C(max)} = 2 · I_{CQ} = [[2 V_{CC}|R'_{L}]]

4.3 Derivation of Maximum AC Output Power (Pac(max))

Under full symmetric swing, peak-to-peak output voltage and current are:

V_{opp} = V_{CE(max)} − V_{CE(min)} = 2 V_{CC} − 0 = 2 V_{CC}, I_{opp} = I_{C(max)} − I_{C(min)} = [[2 V_{CC}|R'_{L}]]

The AC power delivered to the transformer primary winding (and transferred to load) is:

P_{ac(max)} = [[V_{opp}^2|8 R'_{L}]] = [[(2 V_{CC})^2|8 R'_{L}]] = [[4 V_{CC}^2|8 R'_{L}]] = [[V_{CC}^2|2 R'_{L}]]

4.4 Derivation of DC Input Power (Pdc)

The average DC current drawn from the battery is simply the constant bias current I_{CQ} = [[V_{CC}|R'_{L}]]:

P_{dc} = V_{CC} · I_{CQ} = V_{CC} · [[V_{CC}|R'_{L}]] = [[V_{CC}^2|R'_{L}]]

4.5 Derivation of Maximum Efficiency (ηmax = 50%)

η_{max} = [[P_{ac(max)}|P_{dc}]] × 100% = [[[[V_{CC}^2|2 R'_{L}]]|[[V_{CC}^2|R'_{L}]]]] × 100% = [[1|2]] × 100% = 50%

Conclusion: By eliminating resistive DC collector loss and doubling voltage swing to 2·VCC, transformer coupling doubles the theoretical efficiency of Class A from 25% to 50%!

4.6 Transistor Dissipation & Figure of Merit

  • Quiescent power dissipation (idle, zero AC signal):
    P_{Q(max)} = V_{CEQ} · I_{CQ} = V_{CC} · [[V_{CC}|R'_{L}]] = [[V_{CC}^2|R'_{L}]]
  • Figure of Merit:
    FM = [[P_{Q(max)}|P_{ac(max)}]] = [[[[V_{CC}^2|R'_{L}]]|[[V_{CC}^2|2 R'_{L}]]]] = 2

4.7 Advantages & Disadvantages

AdvantagesDisadvantages
Double Efficiency (50%): Twice as efficient as series-fed Class A.Heavy & Bulky: Iron-core transformers add substantial weight and physical volume.
Impedance Matching: Readily matches any speaker impedance (4Ω, 8Ω) by selecting turns ratio.Cost: Quality audio transformers with low leakage inductance are expensive.
DC Isolation: Direct current cannot flow through speaker voice coil.Core Saturation: Continuous DC quiescent current ICQ flows through primary, magnetizing core and causing low-frequency distortion.
Low Distortion: Preserves Class A low-distortion waveform reproduction.Bandwidth Limits: Core losses and interwinding capacitance restrict high-frequency response.
10-Mark Exam Answer Skeleton · Winter 2025 Q5 OR (ii)
  1. Circuit Diagram (2 marks): Draw the transformer-coupled Class A circuit with voltage divider bias (R1, R2), emitter stabilization (RE, CE), and output transformer (N1:N2).
  2. Load Line & Inductive Swing (3 marks): Explain why DC load line is vertical at VCE = VCC. Explain Lenz's Law mechanism that forces VCE(max) to reach 2·VCC.
  3. Mathematical Derivations (3 marks): Derive R'L = (N1/N2)2·RL, Pac(max) = VCC2/(2R'L), Pdc = VCC2/R'L, and prove ηmax = 50%.
  4. Advantages & Limitations (2 marks): Highlight impedance matching benefit and core saturation drawback due to continuous DC bias.
Topic 5

Complete Design Example: Transformer-Coupled Class A Amplifier

Dr. Ghongade Teaching NotesExact Numerical WalkthroughStandard Components
Design Specifications

Target Problem (from Dr. Ghongade's notes, pages 11–13):
Design a transformer-coupled Class A power amplifier to deliver Po = 50 mW to a RL = 4 Ω loudspeaker.
Given: Supply Voltage VCC = 9 V, Lower cutoff frequency fL = 50 Hz, Transformer efficiency ηxfmr = 70%. Transistor power BJT with hfe ≈ 20.

Step-by-Step Design Execution

  1. Calculate Reflected AC Power Delivered to Transformer Primary (P'ac):

    Because the transformer has an internal conversion efficiency of 70%, the primary winding must receive more power than what reaches the speaker:

    P'_{ac} = [[P_{o}|η_{xfmr}]] = [[50 mW|0.70]] = 71.43 mW
  2. Compute Required Reflected Load Resistance (R'L):

    Using the maximum AC power relation for transformer-coupled Class A:

    P'_{ac} = [[V_{CC}^2|2 R'_{L}]] ⇒ R'_{L} = [[V_{CC}^2|2 P'_{ac}]] = [[(9 V)^2|2 × 71.43 × 10^{-3} W]] = [[81|0.14286]] = 566.99 Ω ≈ 567 Ω
  3. Determine Transformer Turns Ratio ([[N_{1}|N_{2}]]):
    R'_{L} = ([[N_{1}|N_{2}]])^2 R_{L} ⇒ [[N_{1}|N_{2}]] = √[[R'_{L}|R_{L}]] = √[[567 Ω|4 Ω]] = √141.75 = 11.9 ≈ 12
    Specification: Step-down transformer with turns ratio 12 : 1.
  4. Calculate Quiescent Collector Current (ICQ):
    P'_{ac} = [[V_{CC} · I_{CQ}|2]] ⇒ I_{CQ} = [[2 P'_{ac}|V_{CC}]] = [[2 × 71.43 × 10^{-3} W|9 V]] = 15.87 mA
  5. Design Emitter Resistor (RE):

    Following standard thermal stability design practice, allocate 10% of VCC across the emitter resistor:

    V_{E} = [[V_{CC}|10]] = [[9 V|10]] = 0.9 V
    R_{E} = [[V_{E}|I_{CQ}]] = [[0.9 V|15.87 mA]] = 56.7 Ω ⇒ Select standard value R_{E} = 56 Ω
  6. Transistor Parameter Ratings:

    Since the device is a power BJT, its current gain is typically low (hfe ≈ 20). Transistor must satisfy:

    • Breakdown Voltage: BVCEO ≥ 2·VCC = 2 × 9V = 18 V (accounting for inductive swing).
    • Peak Collector Current: IC(max) ≥ 2·ICQ = 2 × 15.87 mA = 31.74 mA.
    • Power Dissipation Rating: PD(max) ≥ 2·P'ac = 2 × 71.43 mW = 142.8 mW.
  7. Design Lower Bias Resistor (R2):

    To ensure robust bias stability against temperature and transistor batch variation, the voltage divider current I2 is designed to be much larger than base current (rule of thumb: R2 ≈ 10·RE):

    R_{2} = 10 · R_{E} = 10 × 56 Ω = 560 Ω (standard E12 value)
  8. Determine Base Voltage (VB) & Divider Current (I2):
    V_{B} = V_{E} + V_{BE} = 0.9 V + 0.7 V = 1.6 V
    I_{2} = [[V_{B}|R_{2}]] = [[1.6 V|560 Ω]] = 2.86 mA
  9. Design Upper Bias Resistor (R1):

    Neglecting small base current IB ≈ ICQ/hfe = 15.87mA/20 = 0.79 mA compared to I2:

    R_{1} = [[V_{CC} − V_{B}|I_{2}]] = [[9 V − 1.6 V|2.86 mA]] = [[7.4 V|2.86 mA]] = 2.59 kΩ ⇒ Select standard R_{1} = 2.7 kΩ
  10. Design Capacitors (C1 and C2):

    Determine small-signal BJT dynamic resistances:

    h_{ie} = h_{fe} · [[V_{T}|I_{CQ}]] = 20 × [[25 mV|15.87 mA]] = 31.5 Ω
    h_{ib} = [[h_{ie}|1 + h_{fe}]] = [[31.5 Ω|21]] = 1.5 Ω

    Emitter Bypass Capacitor (C2): Designed so reactance XC2 equals h_{ib} at lowest operating frequency fL = 50 Hz:

    C_{2} = [[1|2 π f_{L} h_{ib}]] = [[1|2 π × 50 Hz × 1.5 Ω]] = 2.12 × 10^{-3} F ⇒ Select standard C_{2} = 2200 μF

    Input Coupling Capacitor (C1): Designed with XC1 ≤ h_{ie}/10:

    C_{1} = [[1|2 π f_{L} (h_{ie}/10)]] = [[1|2 π × 50 Hz × 3.15 Ω]] = 1010 μF ⇒ Select standard C_{1} = 1000 μF
  11. Calculate Collector Voltage Gain (Av):
    A_{v} = − [[h_{fe} · R'_{L}|h_{ie}]] = − [[20 × 567 Ω|31.5 Ω]] = −360
Summary of Final Component Selections

VCC = 9V, Transistor: BVCEO ≥ 18V, IC ≥ 35mA, PD ≥ 150mW; Transformer: N1:N2 = 12:1; RE = 56 Ω, R2 = 560 Ω, R1 = 2.7 kΩ, C1 = 1000 µF, C2 = 2200 µF; Gain Av = −360.

Topic 6

Transformer-Coupled Class B Push-Pull Amplifier

Summer 2026 Q5 (10 Marks)Winter 2025 Q5 (10 Marks)78.5% EfficiencyEven Harmonic Cancellation
Push-Pull Operating Principle

Class A wasted enormous power idling at full bias current. Class B eliminates quiescent current entirely by biasing transistors at cut-off (ICQ = 0). Since one transistor can only conduct for 180° (one half-cycle), we connect two matched transistors in a symmetrical push-pull circuit: one "pushes" current through the load during the positive half-cycle, while the other "pulls" current during the negative half-cycle. The output transformer seamlessly recombines both halves into an amplified replica of the original sine wave!

6.1 Mathematical Proof of Even-Order Harmonic Cancellation

Transistor dynamic transfer characteristics are non-linear, introducing harmonic distortion. Because the two transistors Q1 and Q2 are identical and driven with 180° anti-phase signals, their collector currents can be expanded in Fourier series:

i_{C1} = I_{0} + I_{1} sin(ω t) + I_{2} cos(2ω t) + I_{3} sin(3ω t) + I_{4} cos(4ω t) + …

Since Q2 receives an identical signal delayed by π radians (180°):

i_{C2} = I_{0} + I_{1} sin(ω t − π) + I_{2} cos(2(ω t − π)) + I_{3} sin(3(ω t − π)) + …

Recalling that sin(θ − π) = −sin(θ), cos(2θ − 2π) = +cos(2θ), and sin(3θ − 3π) = −sin(3θ):

i_{C2} = I_{0} − I_{1} sin(ω t) + I_{2} cos(2ω t) − I_{3} sin(3ω t) + I_{4} cos(4ω t) − …

In the center-tapped output transformer primary, current i_{C1} flows downward through the upper winding while i_{C2} flows upward through the lower winding. The net primary magnetizing current inducing voltage in the secondary is the difference between the two:

i_{net} = i_{C1} − i_{C2} = 2 I_{1} sin(ω t) + 2 I_{3} sin(3ω t) + 2 I_{5} sin(5ω t) + …

Profound Conclusions:

  • All even-order harmonic distortion terms (I2, I4, I6) cancel out completely! Second-harmonic distortion, which dominates in single-ended amplifiers, is totally eliminated.
  • All DC bias terms (I0) cancel out identically! Net DC magnetization of the transformer core is zero, preventing core saturation and allowing smaller, lighter magnetic cores.
  • Power supply hum/ripple is cancelled: Any ripple voltage on +VCC enters the center tap and induces equal and opposite magnetic fluxes in both primary halves, cancelling at the secondary.

6.2 Derivation of Maximum Collector Efficiency (ηmax = 78.5%)

  1. DC Input Power (Pdc):

    The power supply provides current pulses drawn alternately by Q1 and Q2. The total supply current waveform is a full-wave rectified sine wave with peak amplitude Ip:

    I_{dc} = I_{ave} = [[2|π]] · I_{p} ≈ 0.6366 · I_{p}

    The total DC power delivered by the DC supply VCC is:

    P_{dc} = V_{CC} · I_{dc} = [[2|π]] · V_{CC} · I_{p} ≈ 0.6366 · V_{CC} · I_{p}
  2. AC Output Power (Pac):

    Let Vp and Ip be peak signal voltage and current across one half of the transformer primary. The AC power delivered to the reflected primary load R'L is:

    P_{ac} = V_{rms} · I_{rms} = [[V_{p}|√2]] · [[I_{p}|√2]] = [[1|2]] V_{p} · I_{p}

    Under maximum undistorted excursion, the peak AC voltage swings to the supply rail: Vp ≈ VCC:

    P_{ac(max)} = [[1|2]] V_{CC} · I_{p} = [[V_{CC}^2|2 R'_{L}]]
  3. Maximum Collector Efficiency (ηmax):
    η_{max} = [[P_{ac(max)}|P_{dc}]] × 100% = [[[[1|2]] V_{CC} I_{p}|[[2|π]] V_{CC} I_{p}]] × 100% = [[π|4]] × 100% = 78.54%

6.3 Derivation of Maximum Transistor Power Dissipation

Total power dissipated as heat in both transistors combined is PD = Pdc − Pac:

P_{D} = [[2|π]] V_{CC} I_{p} − [[1|2]] V_{p} I_{p} = [[2|π]] [[V_{CC} V_{p}|R'_{L}]] − [[V_{p}^2|2 R'_{L}]]

To find the peak output swing Vp that causes maximum transistor heating, differentiate PD with respect to Vp and set to zero:

[[d P_{D}|d V_{p}]] = [[2|π]] [[V_{CC}|R'_{L}]] − [[V_{p}|R'_{L}]] = 0 ⇒ V_{p} = [[2|π]] V_{CC} ≈ 0.636 · V_{CC}

Critical Insight: Transistors do NOT run hottest at maximum volume! Maximum heating occurs at roughly 63.6% of full voltage swing. Substituting Vp = (2/π)·VCC into the dissipation equation yields:

P_{D(max)} = [[2 V_{CC}^2|π^2 R'_{L}]] ≈ 0.2026 · [[V_{CC}^2|R'_{L}]]

Because the load is shared equally between two transistors, the maximum dissipation per transistor (PQ) is:

P_{Q(max)} = [[P_{D(max)}|2]] = [[V_{CC}^2|π^2 R'_{L}]] ≈ 0.1013 · [[V_{CC}^2|R'_{L}]] ≈ [[1|5]] P_{ac(max)}
Interactive Simulation · Class B Power & Efficiency

Explore Class B push-pull operation, dissipation peaks, and real-world efficiency with saturation limits:

10-Mark Answer Skeleton · Summer 2026 Q5 & Winter 2025 Q5
  1. Definition & Schematic (3 marks): Define Push-Pull as a balanced circuit where two active devices conduct on opposite half-cycles into a common center-tapped load. Draw schematic with input transformer T1 and output transformer T2.
  2. Circuit Operation (2 marks): Explain anti-phase base drive. Trace current flow through Q1 on positive half and Q2 on negative half.
  3. Four Major Advantages (2 marks): (1) High efficiency (78.5%), (2) zero idle current, (3) complete cancellation of even harmonics, (4) elimination of core DC saturation.
  4. Efficiency Derivation (3 marks): Show Idc = (2/π)·Ip, derive Pdc = (2/π)·VCC·Ip, derive Pac = (1/2)·VCC·Ip, and calculate η = π/4 = 78.54%.
Topic 7

Crossover Distortion & Class AB Biasing

Winter 2025 SDC-II Distortions (10 Marks)0.7V Cut-in ThresholdDiode Thermal Tracking
The Root Cause of Crossover Distortion

In theoretical Class B, we assumed transistors turn on the instant base voltage goes positive. In real silicon BJTs, no collector current flows until the base-emitter voltage exceeds the forward cut-in threshold VBE(cut-in) ≈ 0.6 V to 0.7 V. When the input signal swings between −0.7 V and +0.7 V, neither transistor conducts. This creates a flat zero-voltage plateau (dead zone) at every zero crossing of the output waveform called crossover distortion.

7.1 Why Crossover Distortion is Particularly Harmful

  • Harmonic Signature: Crossover notches introduce sharp corners into the waveform, generating high concentrations of high-order odd harmonics (3rd, 5th, 7th, 9th). Unlike mellow 2nd harmonics, high-order odd harmonics sound metallic, harsh, and grating to the human ear.
  • Inverse Volume Behavior: At loud volumes, the 0.7 V dead band is a negligible fraction of a 30 V swing. But at low listening volumes (e.g. 1 V peak), the 0.7 V dead zone represents 70% of the entire waveform! Thus, crossover distortion becomes worst at low volumes—precisely when listeners expect quiet, clean sound.

7.2 Eliminating Crossover Distortion: Class AB Biasing

To eliminate crossover distortion, we must pre-bias both transistors with a small forward voltage (Vbias ≈ 0.7 V per junction) so that each transistor is idling just on the threshold of conduction. When the input AC signal passes through zero, one transistor seamlessly takes over from the other without any flat dead band.

  1. Diode Biasing in Transformer-Coupled Stages:

    A silicon diode D1 in series with current-limiting resistor R4 is connected between +VCC and the center tap of the input transformer secondary. The 0.7 V drop across forward-biased diode D1 pre-biases both transistor bases slightly positive relative to ground. Each transistor carries a tiny quiescent current ICQ (typically 5% to 10% of peak current Ip).

  2. Thermal Runaway Hazard:

    As power transistors operate, their internal collector junctions heat up. For silicon BJTs, the base-emitter forward voltage drops at a rate of −2 mV/°C with rising temperature:

    [[d V_{BE}|d T]] ≈ − 2 mV/^°C

    If a fixed resistive voltage divider is used for biasing, Vbias remains constant while the transistor requires less V_{BE}. Consequently, quiescent collector current ICQ increases exponentially. Greater current generates more heat, which reduces V_{BE} further, triggering catastrophic thermal runaway and transistor destruction!

  3. Diode Thermal Compensation (Thermal Tracking):

    By mounting the bias diode D1 directly on the same aluminum heatsink as the output power transistors, the diode temperature tracks the transistor temperature. As heatsink temperature rises, the forward drop across D1 drops at the identical rate of −2 mV/°C, automatically reducing the bias voltage applied to the bases. Quiescent current remains perfectly stable across the entire temperature operating range.

10-Mark Answer Skeleton · Crossover Distortion & Class AB
  1. Definition & Origin (3 marks): Explain why crossover distortion occurs in Class B due to the 0.7 V VBE threshold. Draw input sine wave showing ±0.7V threshold bands and resulting output waveform with flat dead-zone notches.
  2. Harmonic Impact (2 marks): State that crossover distortion generates severe high-order odd harmonics and explain why distortion percentage worsens as listening volume drops.
  3. Class AB Diode Biasing (3 marks): Draw the Class AB circuit with diode D1 and resistor R4. Explain how the 0.7 V diode drop eliminates the dead zone.
  4. Thermal Runaway & Compensation (2 marks): Explain the −2 mV/°C VBE temperature coefficient. Describe how mounting diode D1 on the common heatsink prevents thermal runaway.
Topic 8

Complete Design Example: Transformer-Coupled Class B Push-Pull Amplifier

Dr. Ghongade Teaching NotesExact Numerical Walkthrough15% Safety Factor
Design Specifications

Target Problem (from Dr. Ghongade's notes, pages 20–21):
A Class B push-pull amplifier circuit is required to deliver Po = 4 W of audio power into a RL = 16 Ω loudspeaker.
Specify the output transformer and power transistors.
Given: Supply Voltage VCC = 30 V, Transformer efficiency ηxfmr = 80%.

Step-by-Step Design Execution

  1. Calculate Total AC Power Delivered to Transformer Primary (P'o):

    Accounting for the 80% transformer conversion efficiency:

    P'_{o} = [[P_{o}|η_{xfmr}]] = [[4 W|0.80]] = 5 W
  2. Determine Peak Primary Voltage Swing (Vp):

    Assuming negligible transistor saturation drop:

    V_{p} ≈ V_{CC} = 30 V
  3. Compute Primary Reflected Load Impedance (R'L):

    Each transistor feeds one half of the primary winding, presenting reflected load R'L:

    P'_{o} = [[V_{p}^2|2 R'_{L}]] ⇒ R'_{L} = [[V_{p}^2|2 P'_{o}]] = [[(30 V)^2|2 × 5 W]] = [[900|10]] = 90 Ω
  4. Calculate Total Primary Center-to-Center Resistance (R''L):

    The total primary winding has 2·N1 turns, meaning total end-to-end impedance is four times that of each half:

    R''_{L} = 4 · R'_{L} = 4 × 90 Ω = 360 Ω C.T. (Center-Tapped)
  5. Determine Transformer Turns Ratio ([[N_{1}|N_{2}]]):

    For each half of the primary to the secondary load:

    [[N_{1}|N_{2}]] = √[[R'_{L}|R_{L}]] = √[[90 Ω|16 Ω]] = √5.625 = 2.37
    Complete Transformer Specification:
    • Rated Audio Power: 4 W
    • Secondary Load Impedance: 16 Ω
    • Primary Center-Tapped Impedance: 360 Ω C.T.
    • Turns Ratio: (2.37 + 2.37) : 1 (or total primary-to-secondary turns ratio 4.74 : 1)
  6. Calculate Peak Collector Current (Ip):
    I_{p} = [[2 P'_{o}|V_{p}]] = [[2 × 5 W|30 V]] = 0.333 A = 333 mA
  7. Compute Total DC Input Power (Pi(dc)):

    Average current drawn from +VCC is Iave = 0.636·Ip:

    P_{i(dc)} = V_{CC} × 0.636 · I_{p} = 30 V × 0.636 × 333 mA = 6.35 W
  8. Calculate Maximum Heat Dissipation Per Transistor (PQ):

    Power dissipated across both output transistors combined is Pi(dc) − P'o:

    P_{Q} = 0.5 [P_{i(dc)} − P'_{o}] = 0.5 × [6.35 W − 5 W] = 0.5 × 1.35 W = 0.68 W
  9. Determine Final Transistor Selection Ratings (with 15% Factor of Safety):

    In rigorous engineering practice, transistors must never operate at their absolute maximum stress ratings. Adding a 15% safety margin:

    • Collector-Emitter Breakdown Voltage:
      BV_{CEO} ≥ 2 · V_{CC} + 15% = 2 × 30 V × 1.15 = 60 V + 9 V = ≥ 69 V
    • Peak Collector Current Rating:
      I_{C(max)} ≥ I_{p} + 15% = 333 mA × 1.15 = ≥ 383 mA
    • Continuous Power Dissipation Rating:
      P_{D(max)} ≥ P_{Q} + 15% = 0.68 W × 1.15 = ≥ 0.78 W
Design Summary

To safely deliver 4 W into 16 Ω: Transformer = 360 Ω C.T. to 16 Ω (turns ratio 2.37+2.37:1, 4W rating); Transistors = Matched pair NPN BJTs with BVCEO ≥ 70 V, IC ≥ 400 mA, PD ≥ 1 W.

Topic 9

Complementary-Symmetry Capacitor-Coupled Class AB Amplifier

Output Transformerless (OTL)NPN/PNP PairThermal Tracking StringCapacitor Reservoir
Why Transformerless (OTL)?

Transformers are heavy, bulky, expensive, and introduce frequency distortion and phase shift that ruin high-fidelity sound. The Complementary Symmetry Output Transformerless (OTL) amplifier eliminates transformers entirely! It pairs one NPN transistor (Q2) and one PNP transistor (Q3) having matched electrical characteristics as complementary emitter followers. Operating directly from a single DC supply, it drives the loudspeaker through a single large electrolytic capacitor Co.

9.1 Circuit Topology & Stage Breakdown

  1. Pre-Amplifier Driver Stage (Q1): An NPN transistor configured as a Common Emitter (CE) amplifier with voltage divider bias (R1, R2) and emitter degeneration (RE1, CE). It provides all the necessary voltage gain to amplify the input signal.
  2. Bias Compensating String (D1, RB, D2): Connected directly in series with the collector load resistor RC of Q1. It establishes a precisely controlled DC voltage difference VB between the base of NPN Q2 and the base of PNP Q3:
    V_{B} = V_{D1} + V_{RB} + V_{D2} ≈ 0.7 V + 0.2 V + 0.7 V = 1.6 V
    This 1.6 V forward-biases both base-emitter junctions (VBE2 + VEB3 ≈ 1.4 V) plus drops across small degeneration resistors, holding both output transistors slightly ON to completely eliminate crossover distortion.
  3. Thermal Tracking Diodes (D1, D2): Diodes D1 and D2 are thermally bonded to the common heatsink shared with Q2 and Q3. When heatsink temperature rises, diode drops decrease at −2 mV/°C, automatically decreasing VB and preventing thermal runaway. Trimpot RB allows precise manual calibration of quiescent bias current.
  4. Complementary Emitter Followers (Q2, Q3):
    • Q2 (NPN): Collector tied to +VCC; emitter drives midpoint node through small resistor RE2.
    • Q3 (PNP): Collector tied to ground; emitter drives midpoint node through small resistor RE3.
    • They provide unity voltage gain (Av ≈ 1), massive current gain (Ai ≈ hfe), and very low output impedance (< 0.5 Ω).
  5. Emitter Resistors (RE2, RE3): Small current-limiting resistors (typically 0.1·RL ≈ 1 Ω to 5 Ω) that provide local negative current feedback, stabilizing quiescent current against device temperature variations.
  6. Output Coupling Capacitor (Co) as Energy Reservoir:

    The midpoint between RE2 and RE3 is biased to exactly [[V_{CC}|2]]. Capacitor Co charges to VCC/2, blocking DC from the speaker while acting as an AC energy reservoir:

    • Positive Half-Cycle: Input drives Q1 collector voltage upward. Q2 (NPN) conducts while Q3 (PNP) is reverse-biased OFF. Current flows from +VCC through Q2, RE2, Co, and loudspeaker RL to ground—delivering audio power while topping up the charge on Co.
    • Negative Half-Cycle: Input drives Q1 collector downward. Q2 turns OFF while Q3 (PNP) turns ON. No current is drawn from +VCC! Instead, capacitor Co acts as the power supply, discharging stored energy through load RL, ground, and Q3 back to Co.
Topic 10

Complete Design Example: Complementary-Symmetry Class AB Amplifier

Dr. Ghongade Teaching NotesExact Numerical WalkthroughCapacitor Selection
Design Specifications

Target Problem (from Dr. Ghongade's notes, pages 23–25):
Design a complementary-symmetry Class AB power amplifier to deliver Po = 1 W into a RL = 50 Ω load.
Determine the required supply voltage VCC, and calculate component values: RC, RB, RE2, RE3, Co, and CE.
Given: Transistor current gain hFE(min) = 50 for Q2 and Q3; Lower cutoff frequency fL = 50 Hz; Small-signal resistance hib = 2 Ω.

Step-by-Step Design Execution

  1. Calculate Peak Load Voltage (Vp):
    P_{o} = [[V_{p}^2|2 R_{L}]] ⇒ V_{p} = √2 · R_{L} · P_{o} = √2 × 50 Ω × 1 W = √100 = 10 V
  2. Calculate Peak Load Current (Ip):
    I_{p} = [[V_{p}|R_{L}]] = [[10 V|50 Ω]] = 0.2 A = 200 mA
  3. Select Emitter Stabilization Resistors (RE2, RE3):

    Standard design rule allocates 10% of load resistance to emitter resistors:

    R_{E2} = R_{E3} = 0.1 · R_{L} = 0.1 × 50 Ω = 5 Ω ⇒ Select standard value R_{E2} = R_{E3} = 4.7 Ω
  4. Select Quiescent Output Current (ICQ2):

    To eliminate crossover distortion while maintaining high efficiency, set quiescent current to 10% of peak load current:

    I_{CQ2} = 0.1 · I_{p} = 0.1 × 200 mA = 20 mA
  5. Determine Bias Voltage Drop Across Diode String (VB):
    V_{B} = V_{BE2} + I_{CQ2}(R_{E2} + R_{E3}) + V_{BE3}
    V_{B} = 0.7 V + 20 mA × (4.7 Ω + 4.7 Ω) + 0.7 V = 1.4 V + 0.188 V = 1.588 V ≈ 1.6 V
  6. Calculate Driver Base Current & Collector Resistor (RC):

    Maximum base current required by Q2 under full positive swing:

    I_{B2(max)} = [[I_{p}|h_{FE(min)}]] = [[200 mA|50]] = 4 mA

    Design rule: Ensure driver current through RC never falls below 1 mA during positive peak:

    I_{RC(min)} = I_{B2(max)} + 1 mA = 4 mA + 1 mA = 5 mA

    Allocating minimum peak headroom V_{RC(min)} = 4 V across RC:

    R_{C} = [[V_{RC(min)}|I_{RC(min)}]] = [[4 V|5 mA]] = 800 Ω ⇒ Select standard R_{C} = 680 Ω
  7. Calculate Total DC Supply Voltage (VCC):
    V_{CC} = 2 [ V_{p} + V_{RE2} + V_{BE2} + V_{RC(min)} ]
    V_{CC} = 2 [ 10 V + (200mA × 4.7Ω) + 0.7 V + 4 V ] ≈ 2 × [10 + 0.94 + 0.7 + 4] = 31.28 V ⇒ Select V_{CC} = 32 V
  8. Determine Driver Collector Voltage (VC1) & Trimpot (RB):
    V_{C1} = 0.5(V_{CC} − V_{B}) = 0.5(32 V − 1.6 V) = 15.2 V
    I_{C1} = [[V_{C1}|R_{C}]] = [[15.2 V|680 Ω]] = 22.4 mA

    Since diodes D1 and D2 each drop 0.7 V, the remaining bias voltage is dropped across trimpot RB:

    R_{B} = [[V_{B} − V_{D1} − V_{D2}|I_{C1}]] = [[1.6 V − 0.7 V − 0.7 V|22.4 mA]] = [[0.2 V|22.4 mA]] = 8.9 Ω ⇒ Use 20 Ω Potentiometer
  9. Output Transistor Ratings & Heat Dissipation:
    • Breakdown Voltage: V_{CE(max)} = V_{CC} = 32 V.
    • Peak Current Rating: I_{C(max)} = 1.1·I_{p} = 1.1 × 200 mA = 220 mA.
    • Average DC Power Input (current drawn only on positive half-cycle, Iave = 0.5 × 0.636 × 1.1·Ip ≈ 0.35·Ip):
      P_{i(dc)} = 0.35 · V_{CC} · I_{p} = 0.35 × 32 V × 0.2 A = 2.24 W
    • Dissipation per transistor:
      P_{T} = 0.5(P_{i(dc)} − P_{o}) = 0.5 × (2.24 W − 1 W) = 0.62 W
  10. Design Coupling & Bypass Capacitors (Co and CE):

    Output Coupling Capacitor (Co): Designed so reactance XCo ≤ 0.1·RL at lowest frequency fL = 50 Hz:

    C_{o} = [[1|2 π f_{L} (0.1 R_{L})]] = [[1|2 π × 50 Hz × 5 Ω]] = 636.6 μF ⇒ Select standard C_{o} = 680 μF

    Driver Emitter Bypass Capacitor (CE): Using given hib = 2 Ω:

    C_{E} = [[1|2 π f_{L} h_{ib}]] = [[1|2 π × 50 Hz × 2 Ω]] = 1591.5 μF ⇒ Select standard C_{E} = 1800 μF

Topic 11

Advanced Topologies, Harmonic Distortion & Thermal Physics

Winter 2025 SDC-II Distortions (10 Marks)Quasi-Complementary Lin StageTHD MathematicsHeatsink Thermal Resistance
Engineering Reality: The PNP Problem

While complementary symmetry looks perfect on paper, in reality high-power PNP transistors were historically difficult, expensive, and fragile compared to robust silicon NPN devices (like the legendary 2N3055). To solve this, H.C. Lin invented the Quasi-Complementary Symmetry topology: using low-power complementary driver transistors to control two identical, rugged, high-power NPN output transistors!

11.1 Quasi-Complementary Symmetry (Lin Topology)

The quasi-complementary output configuration splits into two composite transistor pairs:

  • Upper Push Branch (Darlington Pair): Consists of an NPN driver transistor (Q2) feeding an NPN power output transistor (Q4).
    Effective Current Gain: βeff ≈ β2 · β4.
    Base-to-Output Threshold: VBE(total) = VBE2 + VBE4 ≈ 0.7 V + 0.7 V = 1.4 V.
  • Lower Pull Branch (Sziklai Pair / Complementary Feedback Pair CFP): Consists of a low-power PNP driver transistor (Q3) feeding an NPN power output transistor (Q5).
    Effective Operation: The combination acts externally as an equivalent high-power PNP transistor, but the heavy collector current flows through the rugged NPN device Q5!
    Base-to-Output Threshold: Only ONE forward base-emitter drop: Vin = VEB3 ≈ 0.7 V.
  • Asymmetry & Modern Solution: Because the upper Darlington requires 1.4 V while the lower Sziklai requires 0.7 V, a small diode or asymmetric resistor string is used to ensure identical turn-on thresholds, eliminating crossover notches. Both power output transistors connected to the chassis heatsink are identical NPN types!

11.2 Harmonic Distortion & Mathematical Analysis (THD)

When large-signal voltage swings drive a transistor into non-linear transfer characteristics, the output collector current deviates from a pure sine wave, producing harmonic distortion:

i_{C} = I_{CQ} + I_{0} + I_{1} cos(ω t) + I_{2} cos(2ω t) + I_{3} cos(3ω t) + I_{4} cos(4ω t) + …

where I1 is the fundamental audio amplitude, and I2, I3, I4... are harmonic distortion amplitudes.

Three-Point Harmonic Analysis (Second Harmonic Distortion D2)

For single-ended amplifiers where second-harmonic distortion dominates, evaluate collector currents at three key angles: ωt = 0° (peak), ωt = 90° (quiescent), and ωt = 180° (trough):

  • At ωt = 0°: i_{C} = I_{max} = I_{CQ} + I_{0} + I_{1} + I_{2}
  • At ωt = 90°: i_{C} = I_{CQ}
  • At ωt = 180°: i_{C} = I_{min} = I_{CQ} + I_{0} − I_{1} + I_{2}

Solving these simultaneous equations yields the harmonic amplitudes:

I_{0} = I_{2} = [[I_{max} + I_{min} − 2 I_{CQ}|4]], I_{1} = [[I_{max} − I_{min}|2]]

The Second Harmonic Distortion (D2) is:

D_{2} = |[[I_{2}|I_{1}]]| = |[[I_{max} + I_{min} − 2 I_{CQ}|2(I_{max} − I_{min})]]| × 100%

Total Harmonic Distortion (THD) Definition

When higher-order harmonics are present (using 5-point analysis), Total Harmonic Distortion represents the combined RMS value of all unwanted harmonics relative to the fundamental:

THD = √D_{2}^2 + D_{3}^2 + D_{4}^2 + D_{5}^2 + … × 100% = √([[I_{2}|I_{1}]])^2 + ([[I_{3}|I_{1}]])^2 + ([[I_{4}|I_{1}]])^2 + … × 100%

The total AC power delivered to the load including harmonic contamination is:

P_{total} = P_{1} (1 + D_{2}^2 + D_{3}^2 + D_{4}^2 + …) = P_{1} (1 + THD^2)

where P1 = (1/2)·I12·RL is the true fundamental audio power.

11.3 Classification of All Distortions in Power Amplifiers

Distortion TypePhysical CauseAuditory CharacterEngineering Remedy
Non-linear / Harmonic DistortionCurvature of transistor dynamic transfer characteristics (iC vs vBE)Adds octave multiples (2f, 3f); sounds muddy or congestedNegative feedback (reduces by 1+Aβ); push-pull cancels even harmonics
Crossover Distortion0.7 V base-emitter threshold in Class B; dead-zone at zero crossingHigh odd harmonics (5f, 7f, 9f); harsh, buzzy, metallic timbreClass AB pre-biasing via forward diodes; emitter feedback resistors
Frequency DistortionReactive components: transformer leakage inductance, coupling capacitorsTonal imbalance: bass drop-off or treble roll-offEliminate transformers (OTL); enlarge coupling capacitors
Phase DistortionUnequal time delays across different audio frequency componentsBlurs stereo soundstage imaging and transient attacksDirect-coupled complementary symmetry circuits with flat phase response
Intermodulation Distortion (IMD)Non-linear mixing of two simultaneous frequencies creating sum and difference tones (f1 ± f2)Extremely grating, discordant acoustic noise not musically related to originalLinearized input stages, robust negative feedback, symmetrical topologies

11.4 Thermal Physics & Heatsink Design

The heat generated at the semiconductor collector-base junction must travel through the transistor casing and mounting hardware into the ambient air. This thermal flow obeys an exact electrical Ohm's Law analogy:

T_{J} − T_{A} = P_{D} · θ_{JA} = P_{D} · (θ_{JC} + θ_{CS} + θ_{SA})
  • TJ = Maximum safe semiconductor junction temperature (typically 150°C to 200°C for Silicon).
  • TA = Maximum expected ambient air temperature (typically 25°C to 50°C).
  • PD = Power dissipated by the transistor as heat (Watts).
  • θJC = Thermal resistance from junction to transistor case (°C/W, given by manufacturer datasheet).
  • θCS = Thermal resistance across mica insulating washer and thermal silicone grease (°C/W, typically 0.5 °C/W).
  • θSA = Thermal resistance of the aluminum finned heatsink to ambient air (°C/W).

To ensure the transistor never exceeds its safe junction temperature, the maximum allowable heatsink thermal resistance is calculated as:

θ_{SA(max)} = [[T_{J(max)} − T_{A}|P_{D}]] − (θ_{JC} + θ_{CS})

12.2 Full 10-Mark Model Exam Solutions

Model Solution 1 · Summer 2026 Q5 (a) [10 Marks]

Question: What are the advantages of Class B amplifier over Class A amplifiers? Using suitable equations and diagrams, discuss the efficiency of Class B amplifiers.

Answer Architecture:

  1. Advantages of Class B over Class A (3 marks):
    • Higher Efficiency: Class B reaches a maximum theoretical efficiency of 78.54% (practical 65–70%), compared to only 25% for series-fed Class A and 50% for transformer-coupled Class A.
    • Zero Quiescent Dissipation: Transistors are biased at cut-off (ICQ = 0). When no audio signal is present, the amplifier draws negligible DC power, eliminating standby battery drain.
    • Smaller Heatsinks & Device Economy: Transistor power dissipation is shared between two devices, peaking at only ~20% of VCC2/R'L at 63.6% swing, allowing much smaller heatsinks.
    • Even Harmonic Cancellation: Push-pull operation cancels 2nd, 4th, and all even harmonics, drastically reducing harmonic distortion.
  2. Circuit Diagram & Operation (2 marks):

    Draw the center-tapped transformer-coupled Class B push-pull schematic (Figure 6.1). Show that input transformer T1 applies anti-phase signals v1 and v2 = −v1. Transistor Q1 conducts for positive 180°, and Q2 conducts for negative 180°.

  3. Collector Circuit Efficiency Derivation (5 marks):

    Step 1: DC Input Power (Pdc): Supply current is a full-wave rectified sine wave drawn by both transistors:

    I_{dc} = [[2|π]] · I_{p} ⇒ P_{dc} = V_{CC} · I_{dc} = [[2|π]] V_{CC} · I_{p}

    Step 2: AC Output Power (Pac): Peak voltage across each half of the primary is Vp ≈ VCC:

    P_{ac(max)} = [[V_{rms}^2|R'_{L}]] = [[(V_{p}/√2)^2|R'_{L}]] = [[1|2]] V_{p} I_{p} = [[1|2]] V_{CC} I_{p}

    Step 3: Maximum Efficiency:

    η_{max} = [[P_{ac(max)}|P_{dc}]] × 100% = [[[[1|2]] V_{CC} I_{p}|[[2|π]] V_{CC} I_{p}]] × 100% = [[π|4]] × 100% = 78.54%
Model Solution 2 · Summer 2026 Q5 (a) OR [10 Marks Numerical]

Question: In a Class B amplifier, Vce(min) = 2V and VCC = 15V. Find the collector circuit efficiency.

Step-by-Step Solution:

  1. Given Parameters:
    Supply Voltage VCC = 15 V
    Minimum collector saturation voltage Vce(min) = 2 V
  2. Determine Peak Output Voltage Swing (Vp):

    Because the collector voltage cannot drop below Vce(min) = 2 V, the maximum peak AC voltage swing across the primary load is:

    V_{p} = V_{CC} − V_{ce(min)} = 15 V − 2 V = 13 V
  3. Formulate AC Output Power (Pac):
    P_{ac} = [[1|2]] V_{p} · I_{p} = [[1|2]] × (13 V) · I_{p} = 6.5 · I_{p} Watts
  4. Formulate DC Input Power (Pdc):

    The DC power drawn from the 15 V supply depends on supply voltage and average current:

    P_{dc} = V_{CC} · I_{dc} = V_{CC} · ([[2|π]] I_{p}) = 15 V × [[2|π]] · I_{p} = [[30|π]] · I_{p} ≈ 9.5493 · I_{p} Watts
  5. Calculate Collector Circuit Efficiency (η):
    η = [[P_{ac}|P_{dc}]] × 100% = [[6.5 · I_{p}|9.5493 · I_{p}]] × 100% = 68.07%

    Alternative Verification via Generalized Formula:

    η = [[π|4]] · ([[V_{CC} − V_{ce(min)}|V_{CC}]]) × 100% = 78.54% × ([[13|15]]) = 78.54% × 0.8667 = 68.07%

    Final Answer: The collector circuit efficiency is 68.07%.

Model Solution 4 · Winter 2025 Q5 (a) OR [10 Marks Numerical]

Question: In a Class A amplifier, Vce(max) = 15V, Vce(min) = 1V. Find overall efficiency for (i) series-fed load, (ii) transformer coupled load.

Step-by-Step Solution:

Common Given Data: Vce(max) = 15 V, Vce(min) = 1 V.
Peak-to-peak voltage swing: Vopp = Vce(max) − Vce(min) = 15 V − 1 V = 14 V.

  1. Part (i): Series-Fed Resistive Load:
    • In a series-fed Class A amplifier, the maximum collector voltage cannot exceed the DC supply voltage:
      V_{CC} = V_{ce(max)} = 15 V
    • The quiescent operating point is located midway between saturation and cut-off:
      V_{CEQ} = [[V_{ce(max)} + V_{ce(min)}|2]] = [[15 V + 1 V|2]] = 8 V
    • The AC output power delivered to load resistor RL is:
      P_{ac} = [[(V_{ce(max)} − V_{ce(min)})^2|8 R_{L}]] = [[(14 V)^2|8 R_{L}]] = [[196|8 R_{L}]] = [[24.5|R_{L}]]
    • The quiescent collector current is:
      I_{CQ} = [[V_{CC} − V_{CEQ}|R_{L}]] = [[15 V − 8 V|R_{L}]] = [[7 V|R_{L}]]
    • The DC power drawn from the supply is:
      P_{dc} = V_{CC} · I_{CQ} = 15 V × [[7 V|R_{L}]] = [[105|R_{L}]]
    • Overall Efficiency for Series-Fed Load:
      η_{series} = [[P_{ac}|P_{dc}]] × 100% = [[[[24.5|R_{L}]]|[[105|R_{L}]]]] × 100% = [[24.5|105]] × 100% = 23.33%

      (Formula verification: η = 25% · ((15−1)/(15+1)) = 25% · (14/15) · (7/8) = 23.33%).

  2. Part (ii): Transformer-Coupled Load:
    • In a transformer-coupled amplifier, the DC load line is vertical at VCC (since winding DC resistance is negligible). Therefore, the supply voltage equals the quiescent voltage:
      V_{CC} = V_{CEQ} = [[V_{ce(max)} + V_{ce(min)}|2]] = [[15 V + 1 V|2]] = 8 V
    • The peak AC voltage swing across the primary reflected load R'L is:
      V_{p} = [[V_{ce(max)} − V_{ce(min)}|2]] = [[15 V − 1 V|2]] = 7 V
    • The AC power delivered to the reflected load is:
      P_{ac} = [[V_{p}^2|2 R'_{L}]] = [[(7 V)^2|2 R'_{L}]] = [[49|2 R'_{L}]] = [[24.5|R'_{L}]]
    • The quiescent current required to sustain this swing without clipping is:
      I_{CQ} = [[V_{p}|R'_{L}]] = [[7 V|R'_{L}]]
    • The DC power drawn from the 8V supply is:
      P_{dc} = V_{CC} · I_{CQ} = 8 V × [[7 V|R'_{L}]] = [[56|R'_{L}]]
    • Overall Efficiency for Transformer-Coupled Load:
      η_{xfmr} = [[P_{ac}|P_{dc}]] × 100% = [[[[24.5|R'_{L}]]|[[56|R'_{L}]]]] × 100% = [[24.5|56]] × 100% = 43.75%

      (Formula verification: η = 50% · [[V_{max} − V_{min}|V_{max} + V_{min}]] = 50% · [[14|16]] = 50% · 0.875 = 43.75%).

Summary of Results:
(i) Series-Fed Efficiency = 23.33%
(ii) Transformer-Coupled Efficiency = 43.75%

12.3 Master Formula Sheet

Concept / TopologyMathematical Governing EquationsKey Design Constants & Limits

Transformer-Coupled Class A R'_{L} = ([[N_{1}|N_{2}]])^2 R_{L}, V_{CE(max)} = 2 V_{CC}, P_{ac(max)} = [[V_{CC}^2|2 R'_{L}]], P_{dc} = [[V_{CC}^2|R'_{L}]] Max Efficiency η_{max} = 50%

Class B Push-Pull (Ideal) I_{dc} = [[2|π]] I_{p} ≈ 0.636 I_{p}, P_{dc} = [[2|π]] V_{CC} I_{p}, P_{ac(max)} = [[1|2]] V_{CC} I_{p} Max Efficiency η_{max} = [[π|4]] = 78.54%
Zero quiescent idle current
